The SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE CHAPTER I A NIGHT OF HORROR 

THE girl's voice faltered. "Isn't-isn't there any chance that I could reach Mr. Lontos? I-we're in a most dreadful predicament- 99 The little man who stood before her shook his huge head. He was of an unprepossessing and almost misshapen figure, with short, fat limbs and pygmy hands, baggy clothes and a great, shiny head, bald except for a few yellowish-gray tufts here and there-a man who seemed more like a doll grown middle-aged than a successful lawyer. "I am very sorry indeed, madam," he replied in a quiet, precise voice, "but Mr. Lontos has gone to St. 

Louis on business for the firm which will require several days." "Oh-" 

The girl, very pale, drew an uncertain breath. "If there is any way that I can be of service-" She turned as if in hope. "This is Mr. Partridge, isn't it?" "Yes. I am the senior member. madam, and tell me your difficulty." 

Do be seated,

The girl hesitated. She was slender and of medium 

II

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

stature, simply yet pleasingly dressed, with clear and delicate features, rich auburn hair, and blue eyes which were direct and capable. She glanced toward the larger office, which was dark. In the corridor the bustle of the day had ceased. It appeared that Mr. Partridge, too, had been on the point of leaving the building, for he had taken down a gray overcoat and a massive, broad-brimmed hat. "Perhaps I'm detaining you—” "Indeed, no, no, Miss-" "Miss Bruce," she supplied, accepting the chair which he offered. "You see, I-it was really in the capacity of friend, rather than of attorney, that I hoped to see 

Mr. Lontos." "Oh," he cried, "then you are Miss Gladys Bruce? 

I have often heard Nick speak of you." "We were at the school of oratory together," she said. "And-and it seemed that he was the only one 

I could depend upon to help us now." "Do tell me what is the trouble, Miss Bruce." Mr.

Partridge had been quick to notice her nervousness and pallor. 

Again she drew a long, faltering breath. "My younger sister, Helen, has-has just been through a frightful experience." 99 The lawyer folded his puny hands and was silent, while the din of evening traffic drifted faintly from the street below. "And she is facing a still more dreadful one!" the girl added, meeting his gaze with her deep blue eyes. "I must do something-I must find some one who can help!

A NIGHT OF HORROR 

13 "I should explain first that I am a teacher, and my sister is a registered nurse, having been employed for several months at the Corrington Sanatorium in Salisburyport. I did you-have you read anything in the newspapers about the case?"

Mr. Partridge shook his big head. "There has been very little about it," she said, “and nothing whatever about the charge that is likely to follow. I must tell you what happened from the beginning. It makes me shudder just to think of itso you can imagine what my sister's been through!"

She hesitated an instant as if in repulsion. "The Corrington Sanatorium is a large place, dealing principally with cases of mental disorders. My sister, Helen, has been on night duty there. And when one stops to think that in that whole main building there are only two night nurses, one upstairs and one down, among all those violent patients, some of them suicidal and some even homicidal-the only wonder is that something fearful hasn't happened before! "It was on a bitter cold night-last Friday-you remember? The windows were thick with frost and the wind was howling. There was a new nurse on duty upstairs that night. Since nine o'clock, downstairs,

Helen had been making her rounds as usual. "Those house-doctors and the superintendent, Dr. 

Drago Corrington, accuse her of having gone to sleep, but I know she's telling the truth when she says she didn't. Why, the child is completely broken up since that night-she wasn't even able to come and talk with 

Mr. Lontos-"

Her voice wavered. The lawyer did not prompt her.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"Helen was required to make a complete round of the lower floor every thirty minutes, throughout the night. Usually she was able to do this with a few minutes to spare; but sometimes, when a large number of the patients needed attention during one trip, she was several minutes longer. Once in a great while she fell so far behind that she had to omit one round entirely." 

Mr. Partridge pressed his finger-tips together. "I see," he encouraged. He was listening intently. "At two o'clock on the morning of December twentyfirst, however, Helen was exactly on time as she started from the south wing. The first place on her rounds was the electrotherapy room—that is, when any patients were under treatment there. This room had numerous pieces of apparatus for treatment by electricity and machines for heliotherapy. Among these pieces were two electric cabinets, and patients were occasionally given treatment there during the night, adding further to my sister's cares. "She declares that she left this electrotherapy room at three minutes past two by the clock in the corridor of the south wing. In the room at the time were two patients, both undergoing treatment in the electric cabinets. In that wing there were also seven patients whose rooms must be visited. One of these, a Miss 

Wilcox, was extremely violent and often caused much trouble at night. Then, in the central part of the building, there were eleven patients. "It happened that upon this occasion only a few needed any attention, and my sister was soon in the north wing, where an old gentleman had been making a disturbance for quite a few minutes. His room, un-

A NIGHT OF HORROR 

15 fortunately, was at the extreme northern end of the building, and Helen was occupied there for some time. 

Once while she was there she thought she heard a long cry from the direction of the south wing, but when she listened she decided that it was only the shrieking and moaning of the wind. "Doing what she could to quiet the elderly man, who is hopelessly insane, she left his room and visited another patient in the same wing. This time she heard it distinctly and unmistakably—a terrible, long-drawn scream from the south wing, a woman's voice, as if in agony or terror. "But Helen immediately supposed that it was the violent and troublesome Miss Wilcox who was shrieking, for the poor woman was in the habit of making the most hideous outcries at all times of the day or night, and twice the superintendent had been obliged to transfer her to another building. Instead of rushing instantly to the south wing, Helen completed her duties in the north wing before returning-as she thoughtto endeavor to quiet Miss Wilcox. Never for one second did it enter my sister's head- "Oh, it was a horrible thing, Mr. Partridge! A horrible-" The girl seemed unable to continue. She was even paler than before.

CHAPTER II WHAT HELEN FOUND 

MR. PARTRIDGE scrambled to his feet and brought her a glass of water. She thanked him in a weak tone. "I should explain," the girl said, recovering slightly, "that the two electric cabinets in the electrotherapy room were of different types. They were both of the close-fitting variety which completely inclosed the patient's body up to the neck; but one of them supplied heat alone, while the other supplied either heat or electric current, or both, as required. "The current for the latter was produced by a special transformer, and, of course, was capable of being given in small or large amounts by a mechanical regulating device. But previous to this night, there had been considerable trouble with the electrotherapy cabinet; one patient had been uncomfortably shocked, and another had been severely burned. My sister had reported it to Dr. Corrington. "By the time that Helen returned to the south wing, almost exactly at two-thirty, all of the patients were awake and in a state of uncontrollable excitement. All seemed to sense that something was amiss. And then, approaching the electrotherapy room, Helen met the sickening odor of burned flesh. She entered, and saw a terrible sight. "The young woman in the nearer cabinet-a mental 16

WHAT HELEN FOUND 

17 patient was gibbering and screeching, her face bathed in perspiration, while she fought vainly to escape from the contrivance. It was from the other cabinet that the stench of burning flesh was coming. The woman who was fastened there, a Mrs. Marcia Ludlow, was, as 

Helen perceived almost in her first glance, beyond aid. 

Owing to some defect in the regulating mechanism or the wiring of the cabinet, she had received a current of high voltage and low amperage through her body for more than fifteen minutes-and, we believe, nearly twenty. Besides the shock, she was dreadfully burned. "My sister rushed to the controlling switch and endeavored to turn off the current, but owing to the defect in the appliance she was unable to do so. She was horrified by the accident, and for a moment lost her head completely. She rushed to an adjoining building and summoned the superintendent, Dr. Corrington. He raced in, clad in his bathrobe; and the instant that he saw Mrs. Ludlow's face and smelled the burning flesh from the cabinet, he turned white and cried to Helen: 'My God, child, what have you done?' 66 67 "My sister answered, 'Something's gone wrong with the cabinet again-I told you about it before, doctor-' "She started to show him that the controlling switch wouldn't work. But Dr. Corrington ripped out the connecting plug from the wall, and then obtained a fire-ax from the corridor. He said the patient was too severely burned to risk opening the cabinet in the ordinary manner. He sent Helen for one of the housedoctors, and said that he would have to break open the cabinet. "By the time that Helen returned with the house-

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

doctor, both the dead woman and the other patient had been taken from the cabinets, and the electrotherapy cabinet had been smashed. The head nurse at the sanatorium, a Miss Breen, had come on the scenealso another doctor, not directly connected with the place, but a friend of Dr. Corrington whom Helen had frequently seen there. Immediately all four of them indicated to Helen that they believed her responsible for the tragedy. "Again she reminded them of the defect in the cabinet. ""Nonsense!' returned Dr. Corrington. 'You have left her under the current too long, that's all. Have you been asleep?'' 99 "H-m-m," said the little lawyer, unfolding his hands. "Do you see, Mr. Partridge? Those doctors knew, from that very first minute, that the consequences would have to go either one way or the other." 

He bobbed his head. "You mean, either civil action against the sanatorium or criminal action against your sister." "That's it," the girl replied. "They will charge her with manslaughter, with willful negligence in disobeying the rules of the sanatorium. You see, if they can prove that she did that—” "In that case," Mr. Partridge suggested, "the sanatorium probably wouldn't be held liable, on account of its being a semi-charitable institution." "But think of that poor woman, there for a rest because of nervous trouble-literally held prisoner in that cabinet and electrocuted as though she were in the death chair! And-and Helen blamed for it!"

WHAT HELEN FOUND 

19 "Mrs. Ludlow was not a mental patient?" "No. That makes it even worse. 

"It does from a standpoint of civil liability. There will be a heavy judgment for her death, besides unlimited possibilities for conscious suffering, unless it is shown that your sister willfully disobeyed the rules of the sanatorium. Now, what rule can they say she disobeyed?"

The girl leaned forward. "The rule requiring her to examine patients in the cabinets every thirty minutes. "You see, the medical examiner found death due to the passage of electricity through the victim's body, but he couldn't say positively whether the current became too strong or whether she was left too long. It all hinges on the fact that the cabinet was defective " "And the cabinet has been broken to pieces." "Yes. And Dr. Corrington now denies that my sister ever told him it was defective. He denies it, even though Helen is sure he had already ordered a new cabinet from the manufacturers. "When the medical examiner asked Helen that night, what time she had last observed Mrs. Ludlow's condition prior to the accident, she replied that it was exactly two o'clock by the clock in the corridor. But —and this is the amazing part-that remark was the very thing that has made every one believe her story is false. For upon investigation, the clock in the south wing which she declared she had looked at, was found to have stopped at ten minutes past one." "H-mm," said Mr. Partridge again. "And, a fact even more incomprehensible," she

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

added, "every clock in the building stopped that night!" "What? What was that?" "All three clocks in the building stopped that night, Mr. Partridge."

CHAPTER III TO PREVENT INDICTMENT 

"WHY, what on earth caused that?" "No one knows," was her response. "We can't account for it. It was the strangest thing!" "Did they all stop at the same hour?" "No. The one in the north wing downstairs had stopped at five minutes before one, and the one upstairs at a quarter past twelve." 

Mr. Partridge frowned heavily, glancing around his dusty bookshelves. "I suppose, Miss Bruce, you have absolute faith in your sister's declaration that she didn't go to sleep?" "Mr. Partridge, I'm sure she didn't!" The girl's tone was earnest. "I'm positive she wouldn't tell an untruth to me. The the only thought she had for days afterward was, 'Just think if it had been my mother!" " "M-mm. Couldn't the patient in the other cabinet testify what time she visited the room last?" "No; she is practically an idiot, poor girl." "What about the nurse on duty upstairs?" 

Gladys Bruce shook her head. "She was new; that was her first night, and Helen didn't see her after they went on duty." "I suppose Dr. Corrington also denies having or- 21

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

dered a new cabinet to replace the one which was defective?" "Oh, emphatically. He declares he had no reason to do so." "You don't know who the manufacturers are?" "No. Helen couldn't learn that. But we heard today, through a friendly nurse, that some large piece of furniture or apparatus arrived by express this morning from some New York address, but was sent back by freight without even being taken from the packing case. At least, it was sent to the railroad freight office." 

Mr. Partridge made a note. "That is important, though I doubt if it would lead to much. I—I'm afraid—” 

I-I'm afraid-" He cleared his throat, hesitating. "It seems rather a dubious outlook for your sister, with so many witnesses arrayed against her." "But we mustn't say that!" she cried.

He looked at her thoughtfully. "Your sister hasn't yet been charged with manslaughter?" "No. Her lawyer, Mr. Crane, assures us now that there won't be any open accusation unless she is indicted by the grand jury, which is to be in session next 

Monday. That's why we must do something right away-don't you see "

Mr. Partridge shook his heavy head regretfully. "My dear Miss Bruce, it will be impossible to prevent the grand jury from indicting." "But-but it can't be impossible!" "We may be able to clear her of the charge at the trial, which will be held later," he added, a note of sym-

TO PREVENT INDICTMENT 

23 pathy in his voice. "But as for the impending indictment-" "I must find some way to stop it!" she declared. "I will find a way! You-you see," she went on, her tone breaking slightly, "it's-it's for mother's sake. "She has been very ill for a long time. Helen and 

I have had such a hard time to see that she had proper care. She doesn't know of this yet. She mustn't have to know of it—” "But I'm afraid you don't understand just how a grand jury indictment is returned. The person accused has no opportunity to offer any defense. It's entirely a one-sided hearing, a preliminary hearing. "And I really think," he concluded, "judging from the facts as you have described them, that there is ample evidence for indictment."

She looked across the table at him desperately. "That's why I hoped to find Mr. Nick Lontos. I was sure he could help in some way-could suggest something-"

Mr. Partridge nodded in a grave manner. "Yes; Nick could help if any one could," he agreed. "He is fully as proficient as an investigator as he is in law practice, and I know how eager he'd be to do anything he could." 

He paused, thinking. "By George!" he exclaimed, suddenly, "I know the very man you want on this case!" "Oh, who, Mr. Partridge?" "One of Nick's friends. His name is Kendall Mc- 

Arthur, although in this city he's better known as Dizzy

McArthur. He's a private investigator, and also an

inventor of electrical appliances. He's patented an electric car switch, an automatic train control device, and other things. Don't you see, he'd be the very one to look into the matter of the defective cabinet." "Why, of course he would!" She was eager. "Have you any idea what has been done with the débris, the remains of the broken cabinet?" 

The girl's smile faded. "We don't know that," she said. "H-mm. If this McArthur could find it and examine it-" "Do let's arrange with him if we can, Mr. Partridge.' 99 Bounding to his feet, the lawyer consulted an index card on his desk and called a number. He turned to the girl, wagging his head in disappointment. "Mr. McArthur isn't expected home to-night. But see here! I know better!" He stopped abruptly, gaping at the wall for an instant.

Palace-you know where that is?" "But-are you sure Mr. McArthur will be there?"

25 "Beg pardon? Oh, yes, it's right there on the card on the wall," he explained absently, searching among papers on his desk. 

When Gladys looked at the card, however, she was as puzzled as before. It was a printed card; and its only possible reference to that evening was the following cryptic phrase:

December 28. New Haven at Independents.

CHAPTER IV DIZZY MC ARTHUR 

At ten minutes before eight, Gladys Bruce had found her way up-town to the Ice Palace, and had sought a corner in the crowded lobby. Two long lines had formed before the ticket windows and extended far out along the sidewalk; and, from snatches of conversation which she overheard, the girl decided that a skating carnival or some other form of winter entertainment was being held.

Many of those who were thronging into the building carried skates. Gladys was puzzled by the apparent necessity of coming to such a gathering in order to find an investigator.

She was conscious of a sense of hopelessness, of futility. Among all this boisterous crowd, swarming pleasure-bent through the wide doors, she felt that she was an outsider.

At five minutes after the hour Mr. Partridge arrived, his midget figure more ridiculous than ever as he fought his way through the crush at the entrance, with his great, broad-brimmed hat jammed down. Catching sight of the girl, he darted forward. "Confound it!" he muttered, "they wouldn't let me in at the back door-not even when I told them my name!" "Then what shall we do?" she asked, anxiously. "We'll have to get tickets and see Mr. Mac inside. 26

DIZZY MCARTHUR 

27 But we don't need to stand in line; I'll speak to Mr. 

Black, the manager."

The latter was very busy, but spared an instant to accommodate Mr. Partridge. He sent an usher to the box office, and presently the lawyer escorted Gladys through a warmer inner lobby to a vast, roofed arena surrounded by a mounting oval of seats, into which the spectators were threading their way. The reservations provided by Mr. Black were near the middle of the structure, affording an excellent view of the surface below, where a few puddles of water were rapidly freezing on the wide, smooth sheet of ice. 

At one end of the enclosure was an elevated bandstand, and after a few minutes the musicians filed in and promptly began playing a popular selection. Mr. 

Partridge glanced around critically at the long, oval rows of seats above and below, which were gradually becoming dotted with patches of life and color. "Not such a bad crowd at all," he opined. "Better than I expected, considering—" "When do we meet Mr. McArthur?" the girl ventured. "Oh, we'll have to wait until a little later, I'm afraid. 

He won't be at liberty for an hour or two. You seeah!" He broke off suddenly and leaned forward in his seat with keen interest.

A ripple of applause swept over the big auditorium. 

From a gateway below, a band of men on skates, all attired in dazzling red and white striped uniforms, had emerged upon the ice. Each man carried a curved stick, and they began driving a black rubber disk around the ice with great force. Simultaneously, attendants

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

appeared at either end of the rink and set up a pair of cages made of iron and string netting. "W-why-this is a hockey game, isn't it, Mr. Partridge?" "Oh, yes yes. Certainly; I thought I had explained to you. These are the New Havens out here now. 

They're really a bunch of second-rate Canadian players. 

They'll outclass the local fellows, however-all except 

Mr. Mac." "Oh! Do you mean that Mr. McArthur is one of the players?" "Certainly!" The lawyer stared at her. "Didn't you know that? Dizzy McArthur is one of the few really great American hockey players. It's incomprehensible to me why he plays with a gang of dubs like the Independents. They're the joke team of the league! "Our other local team, the Athletic Club, have tried to sign Mac. They have a good chance for the national championship. But he sticks with the Independentsand, Lord!—they haven't won a single game yet! Just one tie, that's all. Here they are now!"

A team in dull blue suits had skated on, led by a bald-headed man of forty attired in massive pads, who took a position in front of the netting at one end and endeavored by various antics and contortions to keep the rubber disk from finding its way into the mouth of the cage. Then two men in white sweaters appeared, and one blew a whistle. 

Mr. Partridge soon pointed out Dizzy McArthur.

But there was no need of it. He was the most conspicuous, the most closely watched man on the ice. 

His dashing black mustache and his baseball cap-both

DIZZY MCARTHUR 

29 oddities in semi-professional hockey-marked him constantly, as did the reactions of the crowd, who mingled applause with jeers and catcalls at his every move. 

The Independents appeared to be unpopular, even at home. When the red and white forwards flashed through for their first score, a pretty combination goal, a lusty shout went up, and Mr. Partridge glumly remarked that his prediction had been correct. 

There was a new face-off, and McArthur secured the puck. He swung behind his own goal and charged up the ice, a flying, white-capped figure. Two of the visitors lunged upon him and crashed him against the boards at the side of the rink. He rose blinking, stole the disk from them again, and drove it from his stick with incredible force. A little, padded Canadian at the cage flung himself sidewise and downward like a disjointed dummy. The puck spanked his flying skate and glanced aside. A vast "Oo-00-00-ooh!" burst from the crowd at the narrow escape. 

But as the three bruising periods went on, the local players tired rapidly. Their efforts became disorganized. Only the amazing agility of the bald goal-keeper, whose antics weren't nearly so laughable in the heat of battle, saved them from being overwhelmed. Not another score was made until the final minutes. 

Then the home goal-tend, his smooth head adrip with perspiration, stopped a shot, slipped, fought for his balance, lost it, and kicked the rubber into his own. sanctum.

The crowd roared with mirth. 

Dizzy McArthur, skating up, paused to remove his baseball cap for an instant, carefully smoothing first his

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

hair and then his mustache. He replaced his cap, patted the chagrined goalie on the shoulder, and laughed with the throng.

The spectators, however, declined to be joined by him. Their amusement merged into a round of hooting and booing. "Put Mac out! Out!" a fat man near Gladys screamed. "Why do they jeer him so?" she asked her escort. "Because he's so aggressive," Mr. Partridge explained. 

At length a gong ended the game. While Gladys waited in the lobby, the attorney descended a flight of stairs leading to the club-rooms. He returned after a few minutes, smiling. "It's all arranged," he said. "Mr. Mac will be glad to meet us and hear your case, Miss Bruce. We're going to meet him in Iceland." "I-beg pardon?" "In Iceland, a café just across the street. Haven't you been there?"

A gracious Eskimo met them at the door and led them to a table near the musicians. There was a tiny dance floor with railings covered by snow which seemed real, and the light was supplied from a score of sparkling and flashing icicles hanging from the ceiling.

Across the room, a polar bear grinned at them from a pedestal, and a walrus's head adorned a niche above. their table. 

Mr. Partridge ordered a light supper for three. Before the waitress had returned, a man of thirty, in a fur overcoat and a fashionably tailored suit of dark

DIZZY MCARTHUR 

31 serge, advanced to the table. He seemed so dignified and austere in his distinctive clothes, so much less robust than he had appeared on the ice with a group of striped opponents fighting to trip him headlong or to bump him against the boards, that Gladys would scarcely have recognized him except by his fine, dark eyebrows and his mustache.

Dizzy McArthur acknowledged the lawyer's introduction with old-fashioned courtesy. Gladys, to whom the whole evening seemed merely an interruption of her own stern necessity, endeavored to compliment the newcomer upon his play in the game. He blinked, winking his bright, dark eyes very rapidly. "Hockey is a great sport," he agreed. "Mr. McArthur, besides being a hockey player, is an inventor and a detective," Mr. Partridge said, “and it is in the latter two capacities that I hope he can help us this evening." "Yes; Mr. Partridge tells me you have a peculiar case, Miss Bruce," the younger man replied with interest.

The waitress returned with their orders. When the food had been served, Gladys began, as she had begun earlier for Mr. Partridge, describing what had happened at the Corrington Sanatorium. Occasionally, at a word from the lawyer, she paused to explain more carefully certain of the details.

She was annoyed as well as disheartened because the inventor did not pay closer attention. Far from asking questions of his own, he appeared positively disinterested, twice glancing aside to nod a greeting to friends at other tables, once scowling and blinking at a heavy,

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

dark-eyed man who sat with two women, and once complaining to the waitress that his coffee was cold.

Finally Gladys paused, openly offended, and Mr. 

Partridge found it necessary to elicit the rest of the story from her by a process of direct examination. "You see, it's a most unfortunate case, Mr. Mac," the lawyer declared, when Gladys finished. "Miss 

Bruce's hope, I may say, was to obtain something which might forestall an indictment. A most disheartening prospect, of course, but-" 

McArthur smiled. "I should call it a most inviting prospect. The matter interests me very much, Miss Bruce, and I shall be glad to try to assist you if you care to have me, in establishing the fact that Dr. Corrington was aware of the defect in the electrotherapy cabinet and had ordered a new one." 

The girl looked at him in astonishment.

CHAPTER V THE NIGHT RIDE 

"OH, then you have an idea about it already!" Mr. 

Partridge exclaimed. 

Dizzy McArthur shook his head. "When I hear anything," he replied, "I can never adjust myself to it for some time. My brain isn't elastic enough. I have to let the thing rest in my head for a day or two, until the rough edges begin to stick out."

He paused for an instant, looking thoughtfully at the table. "I should think there might be a chance of getting corroborative testimony from the patient who was burned previously in this cabinet, or from the one who was shocked." 

The girl regarded him earnestly. "But it so happens, Mr. McArthur, that the lady who was burned never really knew that she was burned. She was supposed to be treated for a severe skin eruption. My sister knew that they were burns, but she wasn't allowed to treat them. That was done by another nurse." "Who is the other nurse?" "She is no longer at the sanatorium. She left on the morning after Mrs. Ludlow's death-left very hurriedly, and we haven't been able to learn anything about where she went." 33

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"What about the one who was shocked?" Mr. 

Partridge asked. "A mental case. Her statement would be valueless." "Humph. Dear me! A stone wall, I'm afraid."

As they dashed over the bridge, Gladys could not escape a realization that the undertaking which meant so much in her own life was merely another game to Dizzy McArthur. She sat in silence as they fled along the avenue, until the long, cold, open stretch of the famous Pine Tree Turnpike began.

It was then that she first comprehended why her companion had been confident of reaching his goal in time. 

Out here on the lonely road the voice of the motor took on a stronger, sterner tone; an insistent vibration crept through the car; and on the dashboard the black figure seventy crept around and held steadily beneath the light.

The Pine Tree Turnpike is the strangest road in the East-a marvelous public race track in stretches, smooth, straight, and level-in other places precipitous as a mountain highway, with hill upon hill towering in unbroken sequence-and in other portions twisting and treacherous. Over the whole scene a bright, frigid moon had risen, mingling its rays with the shimmering beams of the headlights; and through this radiance the pale brown road rushed to meet them, mile by mile. 

There were a few other cars on the turnpike. Now and then they leaped into view out of the night like

THE NIGHT RIDE 

39 bright-eyed phantoms and flashed past, or appeared as twinkling red specks to be overtaken. Sometimes their white lights could be seen descending a mountainous grade in the distance; sometimes flickering in the valleys below; or occasionally they jumped into sight at a hillcrest, blinding Gladys, while the roadster plunged forward to meet them as if dropping over the edge of the world.

It was the plunge downgrade which took the girl's breath away-down, down, the motor singing a deeper note, the speed growing wilder-until all at once the bottom was reached and the upward sweep caught the car-like a flying roller-coaster on a shining silver track. But through it all, she made no protest. sat silent, grasping the edge of her seat. The inventor, from his position, was unable to see her clenched fingers; but the strained expression of her lips told him that she was severely frightened. She

All at once his mind flashed him a picture of another girl-one who had sped with him in the same roadster, over the same thrilling, moonlit road-only she had enjoyed each dip and swerve of the wild race, had reveled in it. The little girl of the night, McArthur had called her, because he had known her only at night. 

It wasn't of his choice that it had been so. He had wanted to take her out over the open highway never to return, leaving the glitter and leer of the underworld behind. The great, steep, shining turnpike seemed unusually lonely to-night.

Unconsciously the inventor had slackened speed a trifle. He drew out his watch and held it to the dashboard, then plunged his foot upon the accelerator.

"McArthur, you're slipping-" he murmured. "I-beg pardon?" "I was upbraiding myself, Miss Bruce-a habit of mine," he explained, blinking. "I've lost a minute and a quarter. See there!"

At the foot of a long, winding hill before them, the street-lamps of Salisburyport were discernible.

CHAPTER VI THE FAST FREIGHT 

KENDALL MCARTHUR really was not a reckless driver. He seemed reckless only in the eyes of people unfamiliar with his performances. He knew precisely how sharp a turn his car could take, and exactly how quick a stop it could make without skidding. He was as much at ease while driving at sixty miles an hour as the average operator is at a speed of thirty. He could dart again and again between cars with an inch or two of space on either side of his running-boards-could do this at seventy-without grazing either machine. 

And, while indomitable in defending his own rights on the road, he never encroached to the smallest degree upon those of others.

In driving long distances, he had three guides: the speedometer, the odometer, his watch. They never failed him. He knew every road and by-way, every hazardous turn and blind crossing; and he knew by heart exactly which danger-signs were unnecessary and which warned of a real menace.

Coasting swiftly and quietly down the last long hill, he entered the main street of Salisburyport. Without slackening speed he cut his wheel to the right, turning a corner so abruptly that the girl exclaimed in apprehension. Ahead, at the end of the side street, the red 41

CHAPTER VII THE TRAIL OF THE PACKING CASE 

"ONE telegram, sir," announced little Wing Shinn, shuffling softly into McArthur's study early the next evening.

The inventor was balancing two fifty-watt lamps in parallel against various combinations of other sized lamps in series, and was intently watching the results as shown by an ammeter. He scowled.

CHAPTER VIII CROOKED DOCTORS THE Rev. Horace Tremaine was a man of striking appearance-not unusually tall, but erect and well proportioned, conservatively and immaculately attired, combining a self-confident manner with the utmost dignity. He was perhaps thirty years old, with quick, thoughtful, dark eyes and a curling black mustache. 

There was an undertone of gravity in his demeanor as he stepped from his automobile at Salisburyport and approached the group of secluded white buildings which comprised the Corrington Sanatorium. 

The location of the place, at the summit of a steep, quiet hill surrounded by open country, afforded a wide view of the harbor on one side; and in the opposite direction were other hills, towering higher, through which the light brown ribbon of the Pine Tree Turnpike twisted its way, alive with tiny motor cars which reflected the sun like brightly painted toys. The Rev.

Horace Tremaine paused in his climb, forgetful of his cares for an instant, while he gazed.

An attendant at the sanatorium noticed him there; and it was the superintendent, Dr. Drago Corrington, a heavy, powerful man of forty-five with a close-clipped beard and hard brown eyes, who met him at the door of the main building. 

Dr. Corrington, from the first, was courteous and 52

53 sympathetic. A clergyman whose brother was an architect and whose sister-in-law was on the verge of a nervous breakdown appeared to interest him keenly. 

He admitted his eagerness. "I may say that a large number of our patients are -er-what might be termed charity patients, Mr. Tremaine. So we are always extremely glad to obtain others, and we promise the very best care."

Dr. Marsh, he well knew, had at his command a far-reaching organization. It was conceivable that he, the inventor-clergyman, might have undesirable company on his drive back to the city.

These were for offensive tactics; they weren't to be used purely for defense.

He drove to a filling station. "Drain the tank," he requested. "I want aëro gas to-night."

Out on the black, open turnpike a biting north gale picked him up and bore him along as if on wings of the night. At the slightest pressure of his toe the roadster went hurtling ahead with mad speed. The sweep of the icy wind flushed his cheeks; in the mirror his eyes were shining, winking.

He laughed at himself. He had overestimated Dr. 

Marsh and his friends. No one had attempted to follow. The highway was almost deserted. A few miles from Salisburyport, a solitary motor-cycle officer smiled indulgently as he passed, north-bound. Ahead was a tiny tail lamp-another motor-cycle, this one with a girl in a side-car, which he overtook and left behind. 

Then, suddenly, rounding a curve, all thought of possible pursuit was driven from his mind. 

A light runabout lay overturned beside the road, with glass and cushions scattered; three youths were clustered around it; a girl stood holding a handkerchief to her face; and-McArthur started with dismay-another form was pinned beneath the machine.

The youths were working frantically to lift the run-

59 about. At the inventor's swift approach, the girl and the young men shouted in chorus for assistance. With masterly precision McArthur brought his machine crunching to a stop beside the wreck.

Quick as thought, two of the youths leaped upon his running-board, and one thrust the ugly snout of an automatic pistol in his face. "Out of that car, Jack, and out quick! Get that?” 

McArthur blinked.

CHAPTER IX SPEED-ON DEMAND 

THE inventor was given opportunity for only a brief glance at his adversaries. He saw that one of the pair on the running-board was an Italian or a Greek, that both were typical city gangsters, and that the girl, no longer holding her handkerchief to her face, was of the sort one might expect to find in their company. 

One other detail he glimpsed-the man on the ground now wriggling easily from beneath the supposed wreck -then his ears were the first to catch the sound, borne down the turnpike by the north wind-the popping exhaust of a motor-cycle.

The man crawling from under the runabout gave a warning cry. "De damn cop's comin' back!" the fourth fellow shouted. "He's after him fer th' way he was dustin' it!" shrilled the girl.

One of the youths on the running-board turned instantly. "Get that car right side up and pick up the cushions!

61 job is enough on our hands! Youse turn over the boat and screw! And you, Jack-come on, damn you -I said get going, d'you hear?”

He ducked into the seat beside the inventor, while his ally clung on the running-board. 

McArthur did not hesitate. At the moment, the pistol prodding his side was a convincing argument. 

Besides, curiously enough, his best hope of escape from the situation lay in prompt obedience. The sputter of the motor-cycle from behind was louder and clearer now, but McArthur knew that it wasn't the State policeman who was approaching-it was the man with a girl in his side-car! He threw the car into second speed and pulled swiftly ahead, for it was imperative that the gunmen should be kept in ignorance of this. 

The roadster gathered momentum. In his mirror the inventor was unable to see the three other gangsters at the overturned runabout, but he saw a single bright headlight swing the curve behind. The man on the running-board swore sharply, anxiously. "Come on come on, Jack-step on this car!" commanded the youth in the seat. "Get a little speed out of her, or I'll show you how!"

McArthur himself was a trifle anxious. He opened the hand throttle a few notches. It was inadvisable to put on too much speed, or he would outdistance the motor-cycle, and his captors would be able to force him to turn out into some lonely side road. Yet he must keep far enough ahead so that the pair couldn't see the side-car. Carefully he watched the mirror. 

The only object visible to the rear was the one blinding ball of radiance. Ahead, the highway seemed

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

deserted. The inventor pressed the accelerator a little farther down.

CHAPTER XI A NEW CALL TO ACTION 

KENDALL MCARTHUR, inventor and private detective, scooped the puck from a scrimmage behind his goal, circled the cage, and knifed his way straight up the center of the ice, a pair of maroon-clad forwards poking and slashing at him, one from each side, and another vainly sweep-checking from the front. As he charged past midrink, all alone in his dash, his skates flashing beneath the floodlights, his keen, dark eyes winking and snapping, the vast throng in the Ice Palace rose, breathless, and from vantage points on great steel beams supporting the high roof, groups of youngsters peered down, agape. 

All through the evening McArthur had thrilled them with his rushes-Dizzy McArthur, the greatest showman of semi-professional hockey, his efforts wasted on a team that was the joke of the league. There was no other blue-sleeved player with him now-none to receive his pass-and he struck the maroon defense, leaped, tripped, and sprawled headlong, sliding crazily behind the cage.

A great, taunting shout went up from all parts of the auditorium, for the Athletic Club players were by all odds the local favorites. But there was relief in the shout, too, because Dizzy McArthur, with his amazing dashes, had kept his Independents continually in the 73

battle, trailing by a single goal. As the tumult echoed back and across through the building, he climbed to his feet, blinking and patting fragments of chipped ice from his curling black mustache. CHAPTER XII HOW MC ARTHUR WORKED 

OPERATIONS on the Potter case had begun several weeks earlier, when complaints had reached Nick Lontos that a large group headed by a notorious George Potter, formerly of Chicago, were receiving great quantities of cocaine from some outside source and were distributing it in the city at prices ranging from five dollars to ten dollars per deck-a deck being a small paper packet of the powder.

Acting upon this information, Lontos dispatched Charles Bowen, a private detective of considerable experience, with instructions to make the acquaintance of the leader of the dope sellers if possible. Bowen succeeded, but the acquaintance was of short duration. 

After a few exchanges of pleasantries, Potter, who was a big, powerful man of thirty-five, swung a short hook to Bowen's jaw that knocked the detective against a near-by doorpost, and followed it with a rain of vicious blows which sent him to a hospital with several front teeth missing and a broken nose. 

At the time, Nick Lontos couldn't comprehend how 

Potter had become suspicious of Bowen so quickly.

That mystery was explained a few days later, when a stool pigeon reported to Lontos that one of the 

Potter group had snapped excellent photographs of

Bowen and of Lontos himself, using a camera con- 80

HOW MCARTHUR WORKED 

81 cealed in a shoe box, a contrivance arranged by Alexander Potter, the dope seller's father, whose hobby was photography under unusual conditions. The stool pigeon, who must be nameless here, earnestly advised Kendall McArthur not to go to work on the case. "You've got no chance of getting friendly with Potter-not after him making Bowen that way, and with your picture on the sporting pages. There's six of them guys from Chi here, and all packing rods." "Six?" repeated the inventor, blinking, then raising his fine, dark eyebrows. "A whole hockey team-hey, what?"

He had accepted the assignment to the case. That had been three days after Bowen's misadventure. 

Then Don Forrest came into the affair, with instructions to work with McArthur. The hockey player was given his choice of Forrest or Irving Sweet, a youth who was a year or two older, as his assistant. Both were wholly inexperienced in such work. Forrest had walked into Nick Lontos' office without recommendations and had asked for employment, and Lontos had given him a trial. Sweet had come with strong influence behind him-his father being an intimate friend of Lontos' clients, the directors for whom he worked. 

Sweet was a college graduate, more self-confident than Forrest, but McArthur preferred the latter. 

Forrest owned a small sedan. The inventor immediately sent him to New York to register the car there and obtain yellow number plates. gambling machines, made in Chicago and purchased by the inventor.

On Broad Street, nearing Albion Avenue, he attempted to squeeze between a six-ton truck and a large brougham, failed, and put on his brakes too late. It seemed a toss-up whether he would sideswipe the truck or the brougham, but the latter was by all means preferable. There was a grinding crunch as the cars came together.

The driver of the brougham, a large man with a heavy-jowled face and sullen dark eyes, burst into a string of savage oaths, to which McArthur responded politely. From the registration the stranger glanced into the back of the sedan and noticed the gambling machines. 

His scowl faded. "Oh—you fellows handling them, too? Lot of people making good with them. Chap that makes them used to be a pal of mine." All trace of his animosity had vanished. "What do you say-let's settle this little damage without any fuss? How about it? My name's Potter-here's my registration-"

CHAPTER XIII KNOCKOUT SMOKE 

WHILE at the dope seller's house on his first visit, Forrest had observed a large safe in a side room used by Alexander Potter for his aquarian experiments and studies. The safe to all appearances was there to provide security for the older man's extensive paraphernalia; but the youth had noticed that upon one occasion 

George Potter had gone to it for data to refresh his memory about other manufactures of slot machines in Chicago. Nick Lontos, when he heard of this, suspected that it was also used for storage of cocaine awaiting distribution by George Potter's agents.

Four-thirty was the hour chosen for the detective's second visit, because George Potter seldom was at home at that time, while his father was there nearly all the time, working at his scientific hobby. It was Nick 

Lontos' hope that the safe would be unlocked while the ex-teacher was busy there, and that Forrest might contrive to obtain evidence which could be used in applying for a search warrant and later in prosecuting the drug seller.

It was a rule in all of Lontos' investigations that an agent entering a suspected place must be "covered," timed, by a corroborating witness, so Irving Sweet was sent with Forrest for this purpose. 

The neighborhood where the two Potters lived was 85

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

a respectable one, within the limits of the city. Their residence was a single house, located about one hundred yards from the intersection of South Albion Avenue, where the elevated railway cast its shadow over the thoroughfare. There were no other members of the family, nor any servant; father and son took their meals at restaurants.

There were frequent callers, however, members of the cocaine distributor's gang, and Forrest was alarmed by the possibility that one or more of these might arrive. Or, much worse, there was a chance that 

George Potter had suspected his true purpose since their last meeting, and had warned his father. Nick

Lontos had spoken with considerable uneasiness of some unknown traitor in his camp, who was betraying many of his plans.

Forrest was not a timid youth, but, as explained before, he was quite inexperienced in detective work, and he had been greatly shocked by the treatment given Charles Bowen, another of Lontos' agents. It seemed altogether probable to Forrest that if there had been a tip-off as to his own identity, matters might be carried even farther in his case than in Bowen's, for the reason that he already had been inside the gang's headquarters, and had listened to damaging admissions from 

George Potter's lips relative to his past activities in 

Chicago. Forrest remembered the stool pigeon's statement that there were six of the dope peddlers, all armed.

Thus there was some slight excuse for Forrest's succumbing to temptation when it was offered himthe temptation to adopt a safe, quick method of obtain-

KNOCKOUT SMOKE 

87 ing the necessary evidence, without consulting with Nick Lontos about it-a method which would effectively prevent Alexander Potter from summoning assistance even though he should suspect.

There was no other justification for the youth's decision. He acted swiftly, upon an impulse, thinking of his own safety and of his sister who was dependent upon him.

His heart was thumping at an unusual pace when he parked his sedan with New York registration in front of the Potters' house and stepped to the sidewalk.

His companion, Sweet, had taken a position two hundred yards away-on the same street, but beyond the intersecting avenue-where he could watch the front door without being seen. Forrest ascended the steps, carrying an oblong box wrapped in brown paper. If he should find that Alexander Potter wasn't alone in the house, he wouldn't unwrap it, wouldn't use it. But if the older man was alone- At the last instant Forrest hesitated-shrank from the procedure through some instinctive feeling that he couldn't explain. It wasn't a scruple at the illegality of the act, for he was too inexperienced to have any knowledge of that. It was almost a premonition. 

But he put it aside. After all, there wasn't enough drug in the cigars to do Potter any injury. He had received assurance of that. A few inhalations from one of these cigars would numb the action of the exinstructor's brain-a few more would put him in a stupor which might last an hour. Certainly it would keep him from any suspicion of his caller, if such suspicion hadn't been aroused already—and if it had,

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

the drug would prevent his taking steps to entrap him. 

The youth came back to his decision. If the father was at home alone, he would use the cigars. It was safer. 

His mind visualized what would happen. Already he had rehearsed it mentally. He would present the box to Alexander Potter; the latter would unwrap it, open it, thank him for the cigars. Undoubtedly he would offer one to the donor immediately, and would smoke one himself. The third from the left-the third from the left, as the label on the box faced himthat was the one Forrest must select. It alone was unprepared. All the others were drugged.

Drawing a long breath, Forrest rang the bell. 

For a full minute there was no answer. again, his heart pounding furiously. Then a slight sound inside caught his attention. He guessed that he was being scrutinized before the door was opened. 

It seemed that he was right. The latch snapped back. 

Alexander Potter stood facing him, cane in hand, his bony face cracked into a smile of welcome. "Good afternoon, Mr. Forrest," he said in a soft voice. "I'm glad to see you. Come in." 

The youth hesitated. "Is Mr. George Potter at home ?" "No, he isn't, just now," replied the other. "But do come in. We've been waiting most anxiously to hear from you."

A swift relief poured over Forrest. With it came determination. His chance had come to succeed where older men had failed! His fear of the dope seller's presence, of possible suspicion, apparently had

89 been groundless. He entered, and the naturalist closed the door. "Step right into my private parlor, Mr. Forrest,” said his host.

The young detective complied, while Potter followed, hobbling on his cane, for he suffered greatly from rheumatism. The "parlor" which the latter mentioned was the one that he used for his studies. 

In the center was a huge, rectangular glass tub, filled with salt water, the bottom laid with stones, shells, and seaweed. Upon this particular day it was empty of fish. The older man explained that he had just completed one study and that his next specimens had not arrived.

A large part of his work evidently consisted of taking motion pictures of the fish. He said that he had frequently arranged a continuous film so that his specimens were under the camera throughout an entire day. 

On a table near the tub were great stacks of developed film, which he had taken from the safe for examination. 

Glancing around as he took a chair, Forrest caught his breath when he saw that the door of the safe was ajar. It was now or never! "Well, what news about the slot machine situation?" 

Alexander Potter ventured softly, relighting a half smoked cigar. "Why, I think there's plenty of chance for more of them," the youth responded. 91 from the room. A swift dread seized Forrest-the thought that the call might be from George Potter or one of his group-a last-minute warning to the naturalist! But in a moment the other hobbled back, grumbling. "Damn it, no one on the line-a wrong number, I suppose!"

He noticed that his host did not seem very willing, but he walked rapidly to the front hall. The telephone was in a closet under the stairs. Entering, he lifted the receiver. "Is this Maple 2147?" asked a man's voice. "No; you have the wrong number," lied Forrest gruffly.

He hung up the receiver. But he knew that this was only a temporary subterfuge. The bell would ring again. He thought of leaving the receiver off the hook. 

But that wouldn't do, either For some inexplicable reason Forrest was afraid of the call. He wanted to prevent the man in the other room from receiving it. The youth turned. He seemed beset from all sides.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

so he encountered a peculiar, pungent, sickening, stinging odor, which grew stronger as he came nearer. His head became giddy, and there seemed to be strange, flickering flashes before his eyes.

He put out his hand to the wall for support, conquered the spasm after a few seconds, and lurched forward to the threshold.

The first object that met his gaze was the cigar which the naturalist had selected from the box. It lay on the rug, burning it, near the cane, which also had fallen. 

The former instructor sat half-sprawled, half-huddled in his chair beside the table and the big glass tub. He was motionless, apparently overcome by the fumes which still choked Forrest.

Something in the other's attitude, in the inert, loose sag of his lean jaw, made the youth uneasy. It frightened him. In a dazed way he moved closer, caught at the man's wrist, held it a moment, his brain now crying aloud, protesting. He recoiled as the full truth struck home. 

Alexander Potter was dead.

what will upset them completely. Don't fold up just because they've scored. Take the offensive."

CHAPTER XVII THE MAN BEHIND POTTER 

"Now," confided Nick Lontos, leaning forward earnestly, "I have obtained valuable information concerning the source of Potter's supply of cocaine. I meant to tell you about it last night, but this Forrest matter drove everything else out of my mind. "My information came to me through a very peculiar channel. Quite recently I found out that Dr. Marsh of this city, who is the dope king of the State and who backs Potter and the various other drug sellers, has conspired to have his wife committed to a sanatorium on the grounds of mental derangement-but in reality to keep her from exposing him in his gigantic dope transactions." "How did you find this out, Nick?" asked McArthur, innocently.

CHAPTER XVIII THE DOPE FIEND 

If there is any single spot in the city which may properly be designated as the center of the underworld, it is the Canton Cabaret in the South End. This picturesque restaurant, run by Wu Leong and Charlie Sang-both long identified with shady enterprises in the gang district-is a gathering place for the worst and the lowest elements from far and near. 

On almost every evening a noisy, strangely assorted crowd may be found there: gamblers, bootleggers, gangsters, "swell" crooks, gunmen; women of the night clubs, of the dance halls, of the streets; dope addicts, pickpockets, shoplifters; and, here and there, chance visitors, attracted by the glamour that has made the cabaret notorious.

There was one occasional visitor to the Canton who was well known there, but who was no longer welcome. 

Many were the ugly glances, mutterings, curses, and words of ridicule directed toward a table in one corner of the crowded room, where a man sat alone. But 

Kendall McArthur was oblivious to it. He sat with his chair against the wall, his heavy, dark eyebrows drawn together as if in thought, a gold-tipped cigarette in his holder. He heard only the music.

Some of the selections carried him back to months when he had frequented the cabaret as an "under cover investigator"-to nights when women had hurled bot- 116

THE DOPE FIEND 

117 tles at his head, gangsters had lain in wait at dark corners, and gunmen had sworn a vengeance as yet unachieved. Other pieces called a slender girl to his table, her dark eyes half mischievous and half wistful-one who had sat with him at the same table in reality, but who would never sit with him again. And still other strains drew him quickly to the present, studying a diagram he had drawn on his menu card, his brain swift and incisive beneath the stimulus of the music, his fountain pen busy.

It was a rough sketch of Alexander Potter's room that he had inked: a cross for the chair, a square for the table, an oblong for the glass tub. On the square he added a box of forty-nine drugged cigars plus one of a quality unknown. Another cigar, not drugged, in the fireplace; two more in a dead man's pocket. Yet persistently the answer eluded him. 

Once again he went over the diagram, filling in other items: the cane on the floor, the letter file on the table, the cigar-lighter close beside the file. Beside it, or on top of it? McArthur searched his memory. 

Then, all in a single, sharp flash of light, he saw it; the picture sprang before him, and his pen poised in mid-air. "McArthur, you clown, it's about time you were tumbling to your senses!"

He tore the menu card into very small pieces, put them in his pocket, paid his check, and strolled out of the cabaret. It wasn't what he had come for-not just for the music and memories to stimulate his brainbut there!-outside on the street corner he was rewarded.

"Me?" she cried. "How could I go in there, when I haven't a dime? I wish you'd take me in, Mr. Mac—” "I suppose the price of the coke is high," he said, with a certain sympathy. CHAPTER XIX DR. MARSH TAKES A HAND 

THE inventor was surprised by the large crowd at the game on Thursday evening. It appeared that semiprofessional hockey had the American public by the throat. Even when the contest was only between a fourth-place visiting team and the local tail-enders, the patrons stormed the doors. Every seat in the great inclosure was taken, and there were many standing. 

Glancing curiously overhead as he came down the passage leading to the ice, McArthur suddenly raised his eyebrows. Seated in the "number twenty box," the best box in the house, was a man of fifty, jauntily dressed, with a tanned, rather rugged face which contrasted peculiarly with his dull brown eyes and his flabby body-Dr. Marsh, promoter of the drug traffic. 

Although the physician held an annual pass at the Ice Palace, McArthur found his presence a cause for wonder. "It isn't often that his highness deigns to watch the lowly Independents play." 

Then the gong rang and the inventor forgot Dr. Marsh.

The visitors were the Chicago Crescents, who wore big yellow half moons on their uniforms and played rough, bruising hockey. Although not yet "in the money" as far as the league race was concerned, they 121

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

had the distinction of being the only aggregation which hadn't been beaten by the St. Croix team of New York.

Upon this particular night they encountered unexpected difficulties.

Whether they themselves were slightly "stale," or whether the home sextet were flashing an unusual brand of play, no one seemed able to say. The visitors simply were unable to make their attack function properly; and, although they were noticeably superior, the first period passed without a score. There was a rather long intermission; then the second session began and progressed in about the same way.

Dr. Marsh was not the only familiar figure in a box seat that evening. Farther along, nearer the bandstand and the west goal, Nick Lontos sat with his senior partner, Mr. Partridge.

The former was unfamiliar with hockey, and was amazed by the players' ability to stay on their skates despite slashing and bumping. "But, tell me! Why do the spectators all shout 'boo!' every time Mac gets possession of the punk?"

Mr. Partridge, whose big, heavy head seemed ridiculously large for his body because of his broad-brimmed hat, hesitated an instant. "That's a Canadian custom of applause, which came across the border to us with the game," was his solemn explanation. "Oh! That's interesting," declared his companion.

A few minutes later, Dizzy McArthur successfully poke-checked an opponent at midrink and came flying down the right lane, crowding close to the boards. A savage slash by a defense player sent the puck spinning

DR. MARSH TAKES A HAND 

123 over the barrier into the throng. Watching for it to be returned, the inventor was suddenly dumfounded to discover his friend Nick Lontos booing him lustily and wholeheartedly!

Just when it seemed that the second period also would end scoreless, a freak of luck occurred. Rondell, the 

Chicago goalie, stopped a shot from little Dick Adams's stick, then lost the puck and it dropped behind him, squarely at the corner of the cage, while he looked wildly for it in every direction. Adams had been crashed to the ice, and McArthur alone was within. striking distance. "Now don't any one say boo-" he breathed, skating up behind the goal-tend. Rondell didn't see him coming and he tipped the disk into the net. 

The gong sounded with the Independents leading. 

This time there was an exceptionally long intermission, caused by the referee, who ordered repairs to one of the goal nets.

No sooner had the last period begun than developments came with a vengeance. Jack Antell, Independent defense man, was sent to spend two minutes in the penalty box for an offense that none except the official saw, and a storm of protest went up from his teammates. Hale was handling the game alone that night, and the blue-clad players detested him. He ignored them, and Antell had scarcely returned to the ice when he ordered Adams out. 

But McArthur's clever "ragging" and "stalling" tactics warded off disaster and used up time, and the game wore into its final stages with the Crescents unable to tally. Then, five minutes before the end, Antell

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

was ruled off again—this time deservedly—and almost at once the referee seized his chance to eject the local left wing, also, leaving the Independents with four men against six.

The inventor blinked. It looked very much as if 

Hale was determined that the Crescents should tie the score.

Getchell, bald-headed goalie of the Independents, strove manfully to defend his sanctum, but the odds were too great; and in a furious scrimmage which piled up nearly all the players of both teams on the ice, scrambling and kicking in front of the cage, the puck was pushed in. 

It was the tying goal; and a half minute later the 

Crescents' center had the winning score on his stick, squarely in front of the net, but Hale blew his whistle.

The Chicagoan did not protest. 

At last McArthur understood. The referee's purpose wasn't to give the visitors the game, but to make it a tie score, to prolong it. And why?

The inventor alone knew why. The thousands of spectators who sat encircling the rink and jamming the aisles had little realization of “inside” conditions at the 

Ice Palace. They did not dream how completely the game and its fortunes-and many other games as well -were under the manipulation of the manager of the place, Tom Black. Occasionally it was done for money, some big bet which was to be swung in a private circle by gamblers. Frequently it was for box office reasons, to keep up interest in a lagging race. This time it was to oblige one of Tom Black's very best friends. 

Dr. Marsh, sitting up there in a high-priced box,

DR. MARSH TAKES A HAND 

125 must have learned of the plans to follow his pet cocaine seller aboard the midnight train, one train or the other. 

It didn't matter just then how he had learned of the plans-McArthur had his own idea about that. The physician, having noticed that Lontos was at the Ice Palace, evidently had voiced his hope that Lontos and McArthur wouldn't be able to leave on the midnight train.

There was some reason, perhaps, why this particular trip by George Potter couldn't be postponed. At all events, it was a safe bet that Tom Black had lent his assistance. There was more than mere friendship învolved. Dr. Marsh was arm in arm with one Stuffy 

McHugh, the biggest of local politicians, and Black was depending upon McHugh to worm a Sunday hockey permit from the City Council. 

Thus it had been decreed that the hockey game should be forced into overtime-into one of those unlimited "sudden death" sessions which might last half the night if one team or the other didn't score. The only restriction as to time was on Saturday evenings, when it might conflict with the existing Sunday sports lawand this wasn't Saturday evening! What galled Mc- 

Arthur was the fact that Black and Hale had never been caught at this kind of thing, had been getting away with it repeatedly.

Sure enough-above the din of the crowd pealed the bell-then a greater shout, for the game had gone into a sudden death period! 

The inventor glanced up at the throng as he skated down to the opposite goal, and all at once a hot surge of indignation swept over him. He had visualized him-

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

self for an instant up there among them, watching the game. This vast assemblage had paid big prices for their tickets; many had stood in line in the cold for hours to obtain rush seats or standing room; the money had gone to Tom Black; and in return these people deserved an even break, an honest game of hockey-and they weren't getting it.

There were a few in the crowd who had watched the inventor in the years before he had become an inventor -when he had swept through all opposition with his great college team. These, perhaps no one else in the gathering-might have sensed the difference as the sudden death period opened, might have sensed that it was momentarily the Dizzy McArthur of old who crouched in his position, with keen, set face and winking eyes.

The referee, Hale, dropped the puck, shrilled his whistle-Adams and the Chicago center fought for possession of the disk-then McArthur was upon the pair like a swooping hawk. With one sweep of his stick he knocked the puck free, followed after it, hooked it cleanly and charged on with great, leaping strokes. 

His own left wing raced to flank him, but the inventor did not pass-he gave Hale no chance to blow his whistle-he plunged full into the defense. 

There was a crash as one man went down, legs flying -the other spun crazily around and swished his stick above his head-and McArthur, reeling sidewise from the impact, bore down upon the padded Rondell, picked a corner, blinked, and slammed the puck into the strings.

It was a goal in eleven seconds of overtime, and the game was ended.

n't have taken the driver who stopped for us first." "But perhaps we can make it yet!" "Not a chance. It's two minutes of twelve. A garage is what we want now-a new drain plug, and eight quarts of oil!"

"To Springfield-or to Heaven," said the inventor.

CHAPTER XX WHEN MC ARTHUR RIDES 

Our through the uptown streets to the boulevard and the start of the State highway to New York, alternately bracing his feet and snatching at the doorhandle, Nick Lontos strove to argue with his companion, first pleading, then threatening, then ridiculing. "Don't be crazy! I tell you, we'll give it up! A man of your intelligence, running such chances on the flip of a coin!" "I'm superstitious," was his friend's response. "You are senseless! With no way of knowing whether we're right or wrong-" 99 Then out on the boulevard, while their speed increased a little with every block, he tried more desperate tactics. "Stop, Mac! Stop this madness! I tell you, I know he has gone on the other train!" "You don't know anything of the kind," returned McArthur.

At an incredible pace they slipped away from the city, its lights, its traffic, and soon the dark, hilly road through the country opened before them, and the inventor settled lower behind the wheel. And from that moment, Nick Lontos didn't attempt to reason with the other, but sat rigid, his knees pressed beneath the dashboard, both feet clamped hard against the floor. 129

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

In front of him a black road was unwinding, twisting back and forth through the range of the head-lights, a road edged with peril at every turning, and it was reeling to meet him with terrifying velocity. 

The powerful motor sang and throbbed like a living thing, now checking with a gentle whine at the crossings, then opening once more in a full-throated, ominous snarl. The metal framework trembled; the dancing beams of light skipped and scurried onward, searching, seeking the way; and the wind, biting cold, wrenched savagely at the curtain and whistled through into Lontos' face, taking his breath.

Into the light popped the red or yellow eyes of danger signals-little sentinels, standing dark until the instant they were needed, all alone in the night. The bridge at Wayland swept into view, a grim reminder of tragedy-its white shape jumped to meet themthere was a rush of wind, a screech of tires-and the Wayland bridge was left behind. Up another hill, down the next, the pace continued, while Nick Lontos hung on in silence, praying that the rim bolts would hold, praying that the body would stay on the chassis. 

Through Sudbury, Marlboro, Northboro, Worcester, then on again into the dark, they dashed-until at Spencer a sedan darted out, almost blocking the highway-there was a scream, and a twitch of McArthur's hands yanked the roadster over, back again, on! "It's ridiculous, the people who are allowed to drive," he told his companion. "An engineer, a motorman, must pass rigid tests even though they have steel rails to guide them, but any half-wit can get a license to spread death and wreckage along the road."

WHEN MCARTHUR RIDES 

131 So the pursuit went on, and the inventor maintained his crouch behind the wheel, his eyes winking at intervals, the night wind on his face, a thrill in his heartfor he had a race to run, the open sweep of the road to run it, a gamble, a hazard to take, and it was for nights like this that Dizzy McArthur lived. 

Then all in a breath, nearing Springfield, they shot out from an underpass to find a beating, clattering roar in their ears; and, glancing up, saw the New York express thundering beside them: first the Pullmans, then the bright coaches, next the baggage and mail cars where a clerk waved down, and finally the hissing, pounding locomotive, until at length the roar of the train grew fainter and was drowned by the wind shrieking past the dashboard.

In the city the inventor twisted the car to the curb and flung open the door. They stumbled out, raced down the sidewalk to the warmth of the depot, and entered as the express rumbled overhead.

Neither McArthur nor Lontos was familiar with the station, and a minute elapsed before they could locate the track. Running up the stairs, they inspected the coaches and the smoker, each from its rear platform, but saw no sign of Potter or of their agent, Bishop. 

Muttering, Lontos led the way down, back to the waiting room.

Here he almost fell over a bright-eyed youth wearing glasses. "Bishop! Have you seen anything of Potter?" "Yes! Quick! Get out of sight!" exclaimed the young man. "He's gone in the smoking room. He met another man right here, a man with a package-they're

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

both in there I'll wager Potter will take the package from the fellow and put it in his bag. Yes! There's the man now-and he hasn't got the package!"

The stranger, a nondescript individual, hurried out to a cab. Nick Lontos turned to McArthur with jubilation in his eyes. "Go and see where Potter is now, Bishop. Don't lose him!"

After another minute the youth reported that the dope seller was seated in the smoking room, presumably waiting for the train in the opposite direction. 

It was enough for Lontos. "Come on, boys!" he cried.

A policeman was yawning and warming his hands at a circular radiator. He caught his breath in amazement as Lontos charged up to him and thrust a warrant in his face. "Our man's in the smoking room!" he directed.

George Potter was actually on the point of dozing when they entered. As the officer approached him he started to his feet as if to give battle; then, seeing the three others and recognizing Lontos, he evidently realized the futility of resistance. "This is a tough week for me," he said in a dull voice, as the policeman placed him under arrest. 

At the police station his bag was opened, a package untied, and Nick Lontos gave a whistle of delight. "Two thousand decks of cocaine!" he breathed. "Is this yours, Potter?" "I'm not saying a word. I want to send for my lawyer."

Furthermore, it will keep right on burning under water, giving off extremely poisonous gases from the combustion, including hydrogen cyanide, which is almost instantly fatal. CHAPTER XXI "WHOEVER SCORES FIRST!" 

THERE was a crowd at the Canton Cabaret. The building, which is on Columbia Street in the heart of the gang district, was aglow with soft, yellow light, and strains of popular music floated out to the sidewalk, where groups were constantly arriving, entering, while others came out in more boisterous fashion and rode away in red cabs which waited in line at the door. On the sidewalk were quieter groups: taxi-drivers who chatted with gangsters; agents, both men and women, who sought to inveigle prosperous newcomers into night clubs or different resorts; and, from time to time, a pair of police officers or a gold-braided sergeant, who watched these activities knowingly, and occasionally stepped in with an authoritative word when one solicitor encroached upon another's "stand." 

Inside the cabaret, the scene was one of hilarity.

Liquor was plainly in evidence, carried back and forth openly; the shouts and wild laughter which told of intoxication rang from all sides. Guests and professional entertainers alternated on the dance floor, and 

Chinese waiters picked their way through the throng with heavy trays.

A man stood alone at the entrance, gazing in at the noisy assemblage in a tolerant, half-interested way. 

He was of unusual appearance-perhaps fifty years old, 137

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

in immaculate evening attire, with hair turning gray at the edges, and with a tanned, rather benevolent face suggesting an outdoor life, contrasting strangely with his dull brown eyes, flabby hands, and sagging body. 

The head waiter caught sight of him and hurried forward, addressing him in excellent English. "Good evening, Dr. Marsh. If you desire a table alone, sir—"

The newcomer shook his head, an almost imperceptible gesture, and returned a thoughtful glance from his watery eyes. "I think that has been arranged. Where is Mr. Sang?" "Mr. Sang-yes, sir. Just one moment, Dr. Marsh."

While he waited, the guest nodded pleasantly to acquaintances at several of the tables. Every one who frequented the Canton Cabaret knew Dr. Marsh. Some knew him only as a genial, lethargic, retired physician, always a good fellow to meet, but not one to invite a close friendship. Others, more privileged, had found his friendship at times indispensable. And then there were a few-a very select few indeed-who knew him as the greatest promoter of the drug traffic in the East. He

CHAPTER XXII THREE WHO WERE TRUSTED 

KENDALL MCARTHUR stood before his mirror arranging and rearranging his necktie and parting and reparting his glistening dark hair. For nearly threequarters of an hour he had stood there, and he had focused three lights to facilitate his task; but still he was dissastisfied, and frowned and blinked at himself, while he worked alternately upon his tie and upon his hair.

Such painstaking devotion to his personal appearance was a frequent occurrence with Dizzy McArthur-particularly whenever he looked forward to some unusual adventure. It was one of the peculiarities which originally had earned him his nickname-this besides his religious observance of the latest styles in clothes, and the wild, heedless, astoundingly skillful manner in which he drove his car.

All in all, he was rather an amazing individual, and he took delight in the fact: amazing to his friends and to his enemies. He was invariably unnerved if he discovered that the tailor had pressed a double crease in his trousers, disconsolate when he couldn't find a fresh handkerchief to match his socks and his tie; but gangland knew him as a man who would batter down the gates of hell itself to attain his objective. 

At last, standing back from the mirror and turning 145

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

his head critically from side to side, the hockey player was content. He put on his waistcoat and coat, brushed the sleeves carefully, snapped out the three lights one by one, and stepped to the door of his living room. 

There he stopped, frowning heavily. Little Wing

Shinn, the Chinese boy who lived with him in his apartment and looked after his personal needs, was huddled in an armchair by the fire, sound asleep. Across Wing

Shinn's knees was stretched Lepidus, a very large Persian cat, also asleep.

Stealthily McArthur took out a pocket ruler and an elastic band, sighted carefully, and released the taut rubber. There was a snap, a snarl, as the big cat arched upward, hissing savagely in Wing Shinn's face; and upon the same instant the Chinese boy also leaped erect, a weird cry on his lips, an ugly, glittering, thinbladed knife in his hand. "For Heaven's sake, Wing," demanded the inventor, irascibly, "what are you up to?"

At sight of McArthur, Wing Shinn suddenly drew back, stuttering and stammering in Chinese, and covered with confusion. He hastily thrust the knife out of sight beneath his belt. CHAPTER XXIII THE GANG LAWYER
