CHAPTER XXIV THE GRAY GHOST DIZZY MCARTHUR was at breakfast the following morning when he leaned forward abruptly and caught up the newspaper, gazing in a dull way at a paragraph beneath a small heading on the back page. "Sanborn to Arrange Quick Sale of Hillard Property-Attorney Warren L. Sanborn, in order to expedite settlement of the estate of " The item was commonplace, but the inventor read it with a shock. "Dad" Hillard dead? It seemed impossible! Only a few weeks earlier McArthur had talked of old times with him, and he had appeared in perfect health. He scanned the paragraph again, but there were no details of his friend's death-only matters connected with straightening a tangled estate. 

McArthur resolved to ask the attorney for particulars. There was no one else to ask, for-despite his nickname-Hillard had been without family or relatives, the last of a distinguished line. Dad Hillard, captain and rover of McArthur's college team-it carried him back ten years at a jump. The Gray Hawks, the sporting world had called them-the greatest American college team in history, undefeated for two seasons, triumphing even over the leaders of Canada.

Certainly there had never been as picturesque a team. 

Clad in light gray suits, almost white, resembling the 159

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

color of the ice itself, with toboggan caps to match, they had flitted and woven their way like phantoms through all opponents, dazzling the goalies with a rain of shots in game after game. 

Three of the team had gone with McArthur to

France-two of these in the air service. Roberts, left wing, had been shot down; Tierney, center, had crashed; Dumaine, the great goal-tend, had made his last defense at the mouth of a dugout where wounded buddies lay. Then Spillane, in training camp near home, had been claimed by the epidemic. 

Since the war, Nelson had followed, in a motor-cycle smash. Now it was Dad Hillard, the captain-and

McArthur found himself the sole surviving member of the famous Gray Hawks.

Another thought came to him. On that occasion a few weeks earlier, Hillard had spoken of his automobile. He still had the same big touring car which he had bought twelve years before-a type no longer manufactured-and when the wheels and radiator had worn out he'd been obliged to replace them by those of different makes. The old motor, too, had given up the fight; and Dad Hillard had renewed it piece by piece until he had assembled one that was practically new and modern, quick of action and of high compression.

The idea of a high-speed, high-compression motor in a car that was geared for an engine of the old type -especially when there were also four speeds forward and wheels larger than the manufacturer had intended -fascinated Dizzy McArthur. It was a thought which wouldn't have occurred to many people-a

THE GRAY GHOST 

161 thought that was typical of Dad Hillard, the greatest of the Gray Hawks. 

Before noon the hockey player had interviewed the executor Sanborn, and found that the automobile, together with Hillard's other property, was to be sold. 

McArthur closed the deal immediately. Naturally, the price of such an antiquated machine, despite the renewed parts, was small.

The inventor took possession of his purchase in the afternoon, and was amazed and delighted with it. The leather upholstery was worn out, the top in bad shape; but the paint was good, a light gray, almost whiteas a reminder of the Gray Hawks, Hillard had said!

The renovated motor, which had been incased in a rubber jacket, was so quiet that McArthur could scarcely hear it unless he pressed the accelerator well toward the floorboard, and then it answered with a quick cold hum which thrilled his heart. He promptly named the car the Gray Ghost, and made one other addition-a full set of new oversize tires. 

He blinked in anticipation while they were being mounted. High-compression motor, transmission and rear axle geared high, extra large wheels, and finally, oversize tires. McArthur detached the speedometer and took it to his special laboratory in his rooms, where he experimented with it, intending to set it that it would register only one-half the actual speed. But he was too late to accomplish this, for Dad Hillard had already done so.

discovered his friend at work on the speedometer and smiled knowingly. "Ah! You are planning to throw a little dust in the eyes of the police, I perceive!" "In the eyes of more than the police," returned the inventor, smiling.

CHAPTER XXV THE COUNTERSIGN 

As McArthur approached the entrance to the club, he counted the red cabs in line. There were six. Coming nearer, he took note of the numbers: 86, 256, 229, 41, 105, and 7. He smiled to himself. By rare fortune, there wasn't a driver in the line who knew him! 

Stepping a trifle faster, before another cab should arrive, the inventor turned in at the lighted alleyway and ascended to the entry, where he was promptly challenged by the doorman. The Silver Star was a private club, and only those in good standing were admitted. 

The membership card provided by Ned Welding, however, proved sufficient. The man returned it courteously and opened a door leading to an elevator.

He knew that the "cocktails" which the hostesses received consisted only of orange juice and ginger ale-although the ones served to the guests were genuine enough. There were two reasons for this: to save unnecessary expense, and to avoid intoxicating the girls and thus incapacitating them.

CHAPTER XXVI THE GAME ON THE THIRTEENTH 

ON Tuesday evening, February tenth, the Independents, joke team of the American semi-professional hockey league, went out of town for a scheduled game in Providence. The contest was expected to settle the "cellar championship"-to decide whether Dizzy Mc Arthur and his oddly assorted teammates were to remain in the last place indefinitely.

On the Independents' eligible list were two new players: Bob Drew, a youngster just out of high school, and Harmon, an old-timer. Dan Donovan, the manager, had reconsidered his decision in regard to these two. Dizzy McArthur had recommended them. The pilot of the club didn't understand the advice, didn't agree with it; but he had signed the players-for he had watched the inventor in the years when he had swept through the college ranks with the matchless 

Gray Hawks, and McArthur's judgment was good enough for Donovan.

The game at Providence was uninteresting from the spectators' viewpoint. Both teams played defensive hockey and waited for the "break," the local players evidently being content to cover McArthur closely. 

Half way through the second period, the break came.

Dick Adams, the Independents' little center, finding his path blocked outside the home defense, took a swift 172

GAME ON THE THIRTEENTH 173 

shot while screened for an instant from the brown-clad goalie's view, and before the latter knew what had happened the puck was in the cage. It was the only score of the contest. 

Donovan's players were jubilant in their dressing room, for the victory left them only a half game from their promised climb out of the "cellar," and they predicted confidently that they would be in sixth place by the end of the week. The manager, however, did not share their optimism, for on Friday night their opponents were to be their greatest rivals, the Athletic Club of their own city-holders of second place, and believed by many to be the best team in the league. 

Had Donovan remained near the locker room instead of hurrying out for lunch with the quickest dressers, he might have overheard conversation among several of the men which would have enlightened him as to the reason for their confidence. The discussion did not start while the manager was present-nor while the new players, Drew and Harmon, were on hand, for it wasn't known just how they would feel about itnor while McArthur and Dick Adams were there, for it was known how they would feel. 

Briefly speaking, the coming game with the Athletic Club was "in the bag." It was fixed: the result was known in advance to a privileged circle. The much ridiculed Independents were to win. 

Chisholm, manager of the Athletic Club, was implicated in the matter; but Chisholm was only a puppet for Tom Black, owner and manager of the Ice Palace.

It had been that way for several years. A crooked referee, Hale, helped Black on such special occasions.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

When Black wanted the Athletic Club to win, they won; when he wanted them to lose, they lost. 

The public never suspected. Tom Black was a familiar figure to the regular patrons. Frequently they saw him standing in the doorway of his office, his quick, shrewd rat's eyes alight with cupidity as he watched the customers leaving the window with their ticketsfor the pasteboards in their hands represented income. 

But the people didn't guess that Black often controlled the fortunes of the games which they paid to witness. 

A gambling ring, friends of Black, were to make big money on the coming contest with the Independents. 

Conditions in the league were ideal for the project. 

The Athletic Club were so far ahead of the Pittsburgh Panthers and the Chicago Crescents that they were sure of second place, yet they hadn't any real hope of finishing first. The Independents weren't even taken seriously, and the gamblers knew that odds of eight or ten to one could be had on the game.

To avoid any possible suspicion, several of the Independent players had been taken into the deal. The referee, of course, would do his part, but he mustn't do too much. Above everything, the Athletic Club players mustn't slacken their efforts to any appreciable degree. They were told to go ahead and score-only the Independents would score more frequently, because several had been instructed to take long shots at every opportunity, and Schummell of the Atlantic Club was to have an "off night" in the goal.

These were the arrangements which the group of players discussed in the dressing room at Providence. 

They felt quite safe in talking after the others had

GAME ON THE THIRTEENTH 175 

gone out. But at one end of the little room a locker door was open, forming a screen; and behind the door was a mirror; and before the mirror stood Dizzy Mc- 

Arthur, adjusting and readjusting his necktie as was his habit.

Of course, he heard the discussion, every word of it. 

It distressed him so that he was unable to manipulate his tie. He said nothing about it at the time; in fact, the others left the room without discovering his presence. But on the next day, Wednesday, he told Dick 

Adams and Donovan.

The former made no comments, but the manager was expressive and to the point. "Aw, hell, Mac-if that's the game, we don't want to know about it-eh? So long as we don't know iteh?” "I'm not sure yet," replied McArthur, blinking thoughtfully. 

Thursday passed, and he didn't see Donovan. He was busy in his laboratory. Friday came, crisp and biting, the ground still bare, and good ice in abundance. 

Hockey was at its zenith, indoors and out. From his window McArthur could see a group of youngsters playing on a small pond across the avenue, using upturned sleds for goal cages.

The inventor had been thinking deeply while he worked on his new electrical appliance. Was it, after all, his affair what others did? Must he forever play the part of a self-appointed adjuster of affairs in which he wasn't directly involved? The problem had begun to exasperate McArthur. Dan Donovan might have

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

known that something untoward would develop when a game was scheduled on Friday the thirteenth!

He dressed carefully and had dinner without arriving at a decision. Putting on his fur coat, he ran out the Gray Ghost and drove rapidly across town to the Ice Palace-and it was then, not until then, that the answer came to him.

He found a parking space for his car and alighted. 

The building was jammed to the doors. Tickets for games between the rival local teams were always in great demand, despite the difference in the clubs' standings. It was well known that Dizzy McArthur and some of the Athletic Club players were out to "get" each other.

Thousands had made reservations before the rush sale. Every seat was taken; the aisles were packed; men and boys were clinging to the big steel beams which supported the roof. Hundreds more were being turned away, disappointed.

Avoiding the crowd, the inventor slipped in by the players' entrance and edged his way upstairs to the manager's office. Donovan and several players of both teams were there-also Tom Black. "I think you'll have to count me out to-night, Dan," said McArthur, simply. "I'm feeling tough."

There was a dead silence, and then McArthur knew that Black understood, that Donovan had said or hinted something. "And me, too, I guess," offered little Dick Adams, moving forward. "I have a lame knee." 

Donovan exploded: "What? Then why the devil didn't ya say so?"

GAME ON THE THIRTEENTH 177 

"Waiting for the cue from Mac, eh?" sneered the manager of the Ice Palace. "We forgot that Mc- 

Arthur is a professional reformer." "I don't happen to be a professional crook, Black," the inventor replied, smiling and blinking at him.

The man's face purpled until the veins stood out. "Oh, is that a fact?" he shot back. "You'd leave me with twenty minutes' notice and a packed house, would you? Well, I'm telling you, you've played your last hockey in this place—and that goes for both of you!"

McArthur did not trouble to remind him that every other club in the league would bid eagerly for his services. He was sorry, heartily sorry, about Dick 

Adams. Adams was an old-timer, too; his wind wasn't much these days; a new contract wouldn't come so readily for him; and he had a family-he needed the extra money that he could earn playing hockey. Adams had played against McArthur and the Gray Hawks in college, had been beaten four times by them-that was why he took sides with McArthur now. 

Tom Black was speaking again. "And get out of this office-the pair of you-and stay out!" he sputtered venomously. 

The inventor turned deliberately to Donovan, who stood in a corner as if bewildered, his mouth open, realizing only that his team must meet the Athletic

Club without an experienced center or right wing. Mc- 

Arthur squeezed his arm. "I'm sorry, Dan—”

That was all. He turned and shouldered his way out to where he had parked his automobile. It wasn't until he was in the Gray Ghost and twisting into the

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

jam on South State Street that he spoke then to himself, accusingly, as he often did: · "So!—you'd play in a crooked hockey game, would you, McArthur, and then drive off in Dad Hillard's car? To hell and be damned with Black!"-and he stepped on the accelerator.

CHAPTER XXVII CLEVER "STALLING" 

THE results of McArthur's break with Tom Black were both immediate and far-reaching. 

Of the former, the inventor learned by perusing the morning newspapers, although he was obliged to read between the lines to comprehend completely. The withdrawal of McArthur and Adams from the game had totally upset Black's plans; for even the dullest of the spectators would have been suspicious if the Independents had beaten their powerful opponents without either of these players in their line-up. The manager of the Ice Palace took the only course possible under the circumstances. He called off the entire arrangement. The final score of the game was seven to nothing in favor of the Athletic Club, and the members of the gambling ring lost their money.

Disastrous as this was for Black's friends, the indirect results of the matter were even more momentous to another group. But of this McArthur learned nothing until some time afterward-two weeks, to be exact -and then in an unexpected and startling manner. 

During the days immediately following the game on the thirteenth, the inventor waited expectantly but vainly for further instructions from Ned Welding concerning the Keenan case. On Tuesday he ventured to call the owner of the private agency by telephone. 179

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"This is Copeland,” he said. "Oh, yes!" the fat man's voice responded instantly. "How are you to-day? Say-I'm mighty sorry to keep you waiting so long. The fact is, we're up against a little delay." He hesitated. "I can't speak plainly over the telephone, but-erone of the judges we expected on the bench isn't sitting yet. You get me? All right, Mr.-Copeland; thanks for calling; and I'll notify you just as soon as the plum is ripe for picking."

The explanation seemed logical, and McArthur gave the matter no further thought for nearly a week. He spent much of the time in his laboratory, experimenting upon a new electrical device, the exact nature of which he was not ready to divulge. Then, late one afternoon-it was the following Monday-a brief message came to the inventor's rooms while he was out visiting his brother. Wing Shinn repeated it faithfully upon his return. "Mr. Ned Welding call, sir, him say you go to Silver

That evening the inventor dressed up with his usual care and drove the Gray Ghost to the street where he had parked it on the previous occasion. He was using the Gray Ghost continually now, instead of his roadster.

Its smoothness and power, its efficiency despite its years of service, had warmed his heart. He had installed a transmission lock by this time, and he secured it before entering the night club

181 For the second time he succeeded in reaching the place without being recognized on the sidewalk. And. upstairs his good fortune continued. Once again, by some strange chance, the manager, Pearson, was absent!

But so was Miss Stella. Frowning and blinking, the hockey player searched the groups at the long rows of tables. He failed to discover the girl. Miss Dora, noticing his glances, approached, but was waved back by the assistant manager, who apparently sensed that 

McArthur didn't desire her company.

When McArthur informed Ned Welding, the latter was vitriolic. "These magnificent women detectives! "I'll get word to her to-day, Mr. Mac, and communicate with you."

Meanwhile, in semi-professional hockey circles a strange situation had developed. The Independents, playing without Dizzy McArthur and Dick Adams, had

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

surprised even themselves by climbing out of last place, thanks to an opportune slump by the Providence club.

Yet in each of their home games they had played to a half empty house, an unenthusiastic gathering.

Dan Donovan, at first, couldn't account for it. The players were openly puzzled. While they had been in the cellar, the joke team of the league, patrons had stormed the doors to see them. Now, when they were in sixth place, with at least a fair chance of advancing to fifth in the near future, and with Harmon and young

Bob Drew exhibiting spurts of high-class hockey, the public seemed apathetic.

Of course, Donovan realized that his team had suffered through losing Adams and McArthur. But could this explain the difference? The manager thought it unlikely, for Adams was a colorless player, and Mc- Arthur was unpopular, the most hooted and jeered figure in the circuit. The

It was Tom Black who arrived at the answer. 

Independents' loss of favor was a vital matter to him. 

Not being a psychologist, he didn't get it through reasoning; it came to him in hard, cold, uncomfortable figures. It dawned at length upon Tom Black's puny brain that the vast crowds which formerly had packed the Ice Palace and had shrieked and booed themselves hoarse, had come for no other purpose than to watch 

Dizzy McArthur lose!

Contrite and apologetic, Black called at the inventor's apartment. McArthur listened, blinking, while the other pleaded his case. "So, Mac, old boy, you can have my shirt if you'll come back to us!" he finished.

CLEVER "STALLING" 

183 

The inventor smiled. "I will," he answered. "But don't misunderstand me, Black. I'm not doing it for you. It's because I don't want to desert the Independents, now when they have a chance to climb. It's the game that invites me back, not your offer. "The American hockey league needs the Independents, a team not controlled by commercialism and greed. And I have a straight tip to offer you, Black.

That was on Wednesday evening. The following day, the inventor failed to receive any word from Ned 

Welding in explanation of Miss Stella's failure to follow instructions. Then, Thursday night, all at once, unexpectedly, Nick Lontos took a hand in the game.

Lontos had been conducting a determined drive against the drug traffic in the city and in other parts of the State. In doing so he had profited greatly by further information secured from the wife of Dr. 

Marsh at the Corrington Sanatorium at Salisburyport. 

One of his agents continued to visit the sanatorium at regular intervals, ostensibly to see another patient; and

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

in this manner Lontos was enabled to obtain evidence leading to a raid upon a supposed grocery store in 

Chinatown. Eight thousand dollars' worth of opium was seized; and Du Leong, a prominent member of Dr. 

Marsh's organization, was arrested.

Here Lontos employed what he considered a brilliant ruse-and undoubtedly it was admirable strategy. 

In order to forestall any attempt to "fix" the witnesses who had obtained the evidence, Lontos caused the names of Kendall McArthur and Charles Bowen to be listed as witnesses on the formal complaint, instead of the younger agents who actually had worked on the case. It was known that a clerk of the court was a "tipster," and Lontos' mistake was intentional.

Of course, when the case came to trial he could offer any witnesses he chose.

It was good strategy, beyond question. Only Lontos didn't realize that what he was doing was the last straw to Dr. Marsh and his associates. He was applying more pressure in a spot where the pressure was already intolerable-and this, under the circumstances, was unfortunate. 

At noon Friday, Ned Welding came to McArthur's rooms. "Confound that girl-she's flown the coop!" he burst forth, easing his great body into a chair. "Gone --skipped from her apartment. We'll see no more of her. Fixed, I guess. Sold out to Keenan. And that knocks the case against him into a cocked hat." 

The inventor sat silent in disappointment. "But I feel that I owe you some work, after engaging you." Welding bent forward. "Listen. There's

185 a big bootlegger up-State, Birdie Zevitas-he keeps two little shacks, one just each side of the State line-and he's running the police of both States to death. McArthur's bright, dark eyes flashed him a look of gratitude. "I'm on!" he said, blinking. "I'll try my luck this very night-after the hockey game!"

CHAPTER XXVIII THREE TOUGH STRANGERS 

A LITTLE round man with a very red face and a bright green sweater raised his piercing tenor voice; and in the hush which descended his tones carried to every corner of the crowded building. "Line-up-for the Independents! Left wing Dr-r-rew! Centah-Adams! Once again the Ice Palace was jammed, for Dizzy 

McArthur was in the game-McArthur, the showman of semi-professional hockey, with his baseball cap and his curling dark mustache-"Dizzy Mac," who spent much of his time in the penalty box, because he invariably picked out the biggest and roughest of his opponents and crashed them against the boards. The evening papers had carried the news, and "fandom" had flocked to pay money for the privilege of “riding" and booing him.

The national anthem pealed out while the gathering calmed into silence. Then the first whistle shrilled, and the throng settled back with a mighty roar, anticipating a wild, rough contest-for the visitors that night were the renowned St. Croix team of New York, 186

THREE TOUGH STRANGERS 

187 league-leaders and prospective national champions. 

They were a brilliant aggregation, in their goldbarred suits a team made up of talented Americans and Canadians. In vain the big, rangy forwards of the invaders tried to turn the home defense; with Harmon assisting Jack 

Antell, the pair were invincible.

Then there was young Bob Drew and his elusive, deceptive speed, worrying the New Yorkers continually.

The visitors were astonished, a trifle upset by the situation. The first period ended scoreless, then the second, also. Up above the rink the crowd wavered, uncertain whether to cheer McArthur and his mates or to hoot them.

And in the final period they continued, playing the league-leaders an even game, until-five minutes before the end-the big break came. Two men crashed together at mid-rink, the puck spun loose, and Bert Kimball, the giant of the visitors, their famous "scoring defense man," secured it and plunged ahead, close to the boards. There was a clatter and crack of sticks, quick and sharp as the rattle of musketry-then Kimball stormed out of the mass, the black disk in front of him. Getchell, bald-headed goalie of the home team, flung himself forward to meet him, but Kimball's shot went high and true.

The St. Croix players fought for time until the gong sounded-and the Independents had celebrated Mc- 

Arthur's return by losing!

CHAPTER XXIX A ONE-MAN TEAM

CHAPTER XXX MC ARTHUR RUNS WILD 

ENGAGING the clutch easily, the hockey player swung his big touring car out of the grounds of the Ice Palace and advanced to the corner, where he turned north. 

At Lyman Street he branched to the right, through City Square, then on through the nearly deserted avenue and out over the wide surface of the North End bridge. 

Of course, there was only one thought in his mind. 

Ned Welding and his group had made a fool of him; they had tried to entrap him, to send him "away."

Such treachery demanded retaliation. And from Mc- 

Arthur's viewpoint there was but a single effective reprisal: a determined attack upon the stronghold of the bootlegger whom Welding's employer was protecting.

He realized that the only sane procedure was to wait until another night, or at least to take some one with him. But McArthur never asked others to share his night riding adventures-not now. Out on the road were men waiting for him; and he had told Welding he would go. If he didn't, the word would spread through gangland that for the first time Dizzy McArthur had run away from them, had shown himself yellow. The inventor's eyes were winking, flashing, as he sped over the bridge. "And they're going to make it look like an accident, 196

are they, McArthur-with four speeds forward and oversize wheels and tires ?"

Leaving the structure, he took the straight road north, the official start of the famous Pine Tree Turnpike, although there was still West Mildon to pass. 

After that, a mile farther now, the "through road" began; the highway had right of way over all others. 

McArthur knew the turnpike, every foot of its forty miles, and he tried to guess the spot where he would encounter his adversaries.

Dead Man's Curve, seven miles south of Salisburyport, would be the place if he were coming in the opposite direction. But to northbound motorists this curve wasn't so great a menace. There were numerous other spots worse, so many that the inventor saw little to choose. The Pine Tree Turnpike is one of the most hazardous roads in the East-straight, smooth, and level at one instant—and at the next, precipitous and twisting, grimly marked at almost every turn with reminders of disaster. 

It was a glorious winter night, moonless, with only a faint breeze, and bright with cold stars. The road had been washed bare of snow by rains, and its pale brown surface stood out sharply beneath his plunging headlights. In the distance below, a speck of color appeared-sparkling red, sparkling green, sparkling red -the cross-roads at West Mildon. McArthur threw out the clutch; and quietly as a gray shadow the big car glided down the hill.

A small cemetery, sheltered by the highway, drew the hockey player's gaze as he passed, and he shook his head, wondering. It was strange that Dr. Marsh's hire-

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

lings had chosen the Pine Tree Turnpike-the road where he, McArthur, had ridden with the little girl of the night and had pleaded with her. And, after all, hadn't his wish been granted, even though unhappily?

She had left the mob of the underworld, had been freed from it, if only to sleep beside the road where they had ridden together.

At the foot of the hill McArthur slackened speed a trifle, watching for the green light, and swept past the crossroads. Beyond this point there were no more automatic lights, no street lamps-only the open, deserted, pale brown turnpike, marked by a fence or a line of white bowlders here and there, a public race track after midnight. With a clattering crash he threw the gears into fourth speed, and the Gray Ghost seemed to glide out from beneath him as he stepped on the accelerator.

Meanwhile, out on the dark, lonely road ahead, sinister preparations were under way. 

The locality chosen was probably the last that the inventor would have suspected-the longest level stretch on the entire turnpike. But it was chosen advisedly, for on this five-mile straightaway from Hopefield to Little River there isn't a single crossroadnothing whatever to restrict the speed-and it was upon McArthur's speed that Dr. Marsh's group were depending for his destruction.

At eleven o'clock, a fleet of four cars had gathered at a meeting place a mile and a half beyond Hopefield. 

At this point the highway had been cut through towering ledges which bulged close on either side; and to the left a very steep, slippery driveway ran up to the

grounds of the deserted Hopefield Bluff Inn. All four of the gangsters' machines turned in at this entrance, and the occupants gathered on the dark veranda of the road house, out of view from the turnpike. 

The man in charge of the party was a lean, wiry individual, thin-lipped and lantern-jawed. He greeted the others in quick, flat syllables; then, taking a flash light from his sedan, examined the three other automobiles critically. 

One was a racing model of the latest type, without fenders or glass to resist the wind. It was evident at a glance that no machine with top and windshield could compete with this car. Next in line was a roadster; and the third was a closed car fit for the junk yard-the roof scraped and broken, the tires practically worthless. Under the direction of the thin man, Cartwright, this battered relic was turned around and left facing down the steep drive toward the turnpike, its front wheels blocked by stones.

Dr. Marsh had smiled when he had chosen the deserted road house as a rendezvous, and he had dryly termed it poetic justice; for Nick Lontos had been instrumental in closing the establishment and in having the proprietor's license revoked. Only on this particular night the place wasn't entirely closed: one room was open, and the telephone was connected-a convenient arrangement for the gangsters. 

The first message arrived at seventeen minutes after eleven. Cartwright, in his metallic tone, conveyed it to the men outside. "The game's over, but he's gone across for chow with a jane."

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

A short, thick-set man, unrecognizable beneath his goggles and turned-up collar, inspected the motor of his racing car. 

The others smoked and talked quietly until the next bulletin came. "The jane went home in a cab. He's started-he turned north on South State Street-wait until Welding's ferret checks him through City Square."

The group clustered around him, businesslike, ominous. Besides Cartwright and the man in goggles, there were five: Eagle-Eye Barney of Philadelphia, a former lightweight fighter; Stripes Colby, a sallowskinned man of thirty who was proud of his prison record; Jim Dole, who had gray hair and a twisted mouth; and a pair of youths supplied by the Castle gang in the city. "Now, youse two boys on this piece of junk know your stuff? We get our last tip from East Houghton, four miles from here; and when youse get my flash, then act fast-but don't make a move till youse do get it! His nibs will steer around this job if it gets down there one second too soon. And don't forget your burned-out light bulbs-put 'em in now. This thing has got to look right, as if one driver beat it afterward because he had no lights or registration."

The young men nodded, as from the open window came the muffled bell of a telephone. "He's over the 

North End Bridge," was the word. "That means he's coming, all right. Now, youse in the cars, ease along. 

One mile for you, Butch-three for the others. If he gets by here, you'll know it soon enough." Cartwright peered at his watch. "He ought to make East 

Houghton in twenty minutes."

The begoggled man, Butch, took the racing car; Jim 

Dole the roadster; while Eagle-Eye Barney and Colby occupied Cartwright's high-powered sedan. In line the three machines moved into the drive and crawled down, turning north on the highway below.

They were to be in readiness for a second attack if the first should fail. Butch in his racing model was to pick up McArthur two miles and a half from the Little River bridge—then to keep close in front of him, refusing to let him pass. On the curve approaching the river, two speeding cars, Butch's and McArthur's, would attempt to pass two other cars, Jim Dole's roadster and the big sedan. The approach to the bridge was well known as a perilous place-only a few months earlier a family had been drowned there—and as for witnesses, the turnpike was always practically deserted after midnight in the winter.

When the three automobiles were out of sight, Cartwright busied himself by inspecting the old car on the driveway, making sure that the brakes were released, the switches at the dashboard turned on. He had stepped aside to confer again with the two young gangsters when he was startled by a shrill cry from the window. "Hey! Can't you hear the 'phone, you dummies?" 

It was the girl's voice. "Jack says he's gone through 

East Houghton!" "What? Why, that's impossible!" returned the leader, snatching out his watch. "The man's crazyhe's mistaken the car-" "Well, that's what he says, I'm telling you!" she screamed.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

One of the youths, Hoaman, whirled and pointed southward along the turnpike. "Look!" His tone was husky.

Far up the long, level sweep of the road, where it emerged from the hills in the distance, a shimmering silver thread of light was creeping. As they watched, it became brighter, throwing a fantastic haze among the naked tops of the trees. "Some one's comin', an' comin' like hell!" Hoaman told them. "God Almighty!" said Cartwright hoarsely-and he half scrambled, half slid down the steep driveway to the gate.

There was no doubt in his heart now-not when he glimpsed the dazzling balls of light as they swung into the straightaway and grew larger, more blinding, each second. Crouching behind one of the stone pillars, he watched, calculating, his finger on the button of his torch. From up the road came the prickling whine of tires; then a deeper hum, monotonous but menacing; and the cough of the exhaust, echoing back from the cold, rocky walls like the ripping of crisp paper-Dizzy McArthur, zooming over the turnpike at one hundred and two miles per hour. 

Cartwright gasped and pressed the button. High above, on the fringe of the dancing light, two shadowy forms leaped into action. Between them a larger mass took shape, moving forward-slowly at first, then quickening, until it topped the crest of the drive and came hurtling, bouncing down. Breathless, the gang leader watched the converging cars, and in a sickening instant he realized that he had bungled.

There was a swish of wind, a louder whine of tires, a twinkle of bright lights flashing past; and the black hulk of the old automobile, plunging out across the turnpike, smote the opposite wall of rock a terrific, shattering impact-then rebounded, overturning. 

At the wheel of the Gray Ghost, the inventor heard the crash. His brain, thoroughly awakened at last, jumped to the explanation. He had recognized the driveway of the road house as it rushed to meet him and was gone-the scene of an investigation by Nick Lontos. 

McArthur blinked. "Very good, doctor," he applauded. "Now, I wonder, is that the only trick in your bag?"

Alert, his muscles tense above the trembling wheel, he studied the road as it unreeled with incredible velocity before his lamps, on his guard for danger from every wooded pocket, from each wall of earth or rock as it appeared and flashed past. His eyes caught a spark of red, which grew slowly larger-another car, racing almost as fast as his own.

As he drew closer he discerned that the machine was a speed model, driven by a man with goggles, who kept twisting glances backward at intervals. Overtaking the other car slowly but steadily, the hockey player signaled and attempted to pass. 

Instantly the begoggled man put on a burst of speed and drew away for a moment. McArthur blinked at the tremendous power of the racing car. But a half minute later it slowed a little; and the inventor, who had continued at an even pace, again touched his horn. 

For the second time the other driver spurted.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

This annoyed McArthur. Such procedure invariably did. He saw no reason to connect the racing car with his antagonists, but he made a third effort to pass, this time without any signal. 

The man in goggles, however, took warning from the flickering white light and frustrated the attempt, pulling ahead easily. McArthur dimmed his headlights momentarily and tried again. At last the other was caught napping; the Gray Ghost slanted across in front. 

The inventor couldn't hear any words, but he could see the man's mouth working. For a full mile-although a mile didn't take long at that mad pace-the stranger used every art at his command to regain his advantage. But McArthur was aroused, and he was a master of such tactics. Thus, a half minute later, when they suddenly bore down upon the curve approaching the Little River bridge, it was the Gray Ghost, not the racing car, that was leading.

Swift as a picture film the scene took shape: the long curve sweeping to the right, the danger signal and the white fence at the left, and two cars just ahead, a roadster and a sedan. To the hockey player every detail was distinct. But in the gangsters' cars the men were blinded-it was only a glare to them, with two white-eyed demons charging from behind. Jim Dole, in the roadster, marked the second pair of lights, gauged the speed, set his teeth; and as the cars flashed abreast he cut his wheel across quick and hard. 

There was a thumping crash, a soggy screech of tires, and the racing model was knocked sidewise, splintering the white fence, then tumbling end over end until it spanked the black surface of the water

below. Eagle-Eye Barney, operating the sedan, noticed the failure of the leading driver to jam his brakes as per schedule, caught one glimpse of the car still abreast, and, comprehending, he crowded across viciously. But he was too slow, far too slow for the Gray Ghost, which slipped away from him and fled northward on an open road, its heavy exhaust-pipe spitting defiance into the night!

CHAPTER XXXI THE BOOTLEGGER'S SHACKS 

MCARTHUR watched the two pairs of bright lights in his mirror: one pair, motionless, knocked awry by the collision; the other holding steadily in the glass, although gradually becoming smaller. The sedan was following, but its best was no match for the "fourth speed forward" of the Gray Ghost.

He raced on, his speedometer trembling close to forty-five-a speedometer trained to tell only half the grim truth! Up and down through the hilly sections, yanking, straining with wicked velocity on the curves, the tires shrieking as he prodded the accelerator on each turn, McArthur set a pace which left the gangsters farther behind every instant, until at length the mirror was dark.

So swiftly that it almost startled him, a glow appeared ahead, at the foot of a long, hard grade-the street lamps of Salisburyport. He chuckled, blinking, as he coasted the last hill at breath-taking speed. It had been a record run this time-there wasn't any question about that-a record even for Dizzy McArthur! 

As his car glided into the town, he threw a glance backward, thinking rapidly.

He believed that he was at least five minutes ahead of his pursuers. Of course, here in Salisburyport he was safe, but the bootlegger's hangout was six miles farther north, at the State line, near Hammond Center. 206

THE BOOTLEGGER'S SHACKS 207 

If he should go there directly, his car might betray him- Scanning a line of taxicabs at an all-night lunch room, McArthur shook his head. Questions might be asked here, also answered. Farther along the main street he discovered a lone cab, painted green. Applying his brakes, he hailed the driver. "Taxi, sir?" the man asked in astonishment. "Yes! Start your clock and follow me up the line."

The driver had difficulty in keeping the pace. But when McArthur had gone a half mile from the town, he stopped beside the road and leaped out. Seizing his flash light, he threw back the hood, and his fingers worked quickly, detaching a screw which rotated the timing shaft. He slipped the screw in his pocket as the cab arrived. "Broke down, sir?" The man was greatly puzzled. "Yes. Take me to Hammond Center as fast as you can."

Looking back, the inventor nodded in satisfaction. 

The Gray Ghost stood at the side of the road, its hood raised; and an instant of investigation would reveal that the timer was out of commission. The gangsters' logical conclusion would be that the occupant of the car had gone for aid to the nearest place-back to Salisburyport.

The road led through open country for five miles. 

Then, as the cab rounded a bend, a light in the distance, suspended over a large stone tablet, caught the hockey player's gaze-the State line. He took careful notice of the houses which they passed. 

All were dark. One, about two hundred yards south

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

of the line, he recognized from Ned Welding's description. The latter had told him he would find a telephone there. McArthur smiled faintly. He hadn't the slightest intention of following Welding's advice!

Beyond this farm there was a wooded tract, which ended a short distance from the stone marking the line. Peering across a cleared area, the inventor discerned a small, one-story structure snuggled beside the woods, away from the road. And beyond the inscribed tablet he saw another, an even smaller house, on the same side of the highway. Both places appeared unlighted. But in the second yard was something which thrilled McArthur-a motor truck. He rode on until the cab reached the outskirts of Hammond Center. "Right here," he directed, taking a bill from his folder.

The driver, a greasy, sleepy-looking individual, extending the change, stopped and stared at a badge in 

McArthur's palm. "Say nothing to any one," the hockey player requested. "I won't, sir," the man promised instantly.

McArthur considered the answer as the cab buzzed out of sight toward Salisburyport. It had sounded sincere enough. The shield that he had exhibited was really only a private detective's "tin," supplied by Nick

Lontos, but it had obviously impressed the driver. 

The inventor peered at his watch. It was still early for the effort-not yet twelve-thirty. Leaving the silent street of the town, he walked back along the open highway toward the tablet marking the line.

When he came in sight of the place, he crossed to

THE BOOTLEGGER'S SHACKS 209 

the fields on the right of the road. It was pasture land bordered by woods, affording him a chance for instant concealment. Once, pausing by a stone wall, he took out his flash light and examined something which he drew from the opposite pocket of his coat. The beam shone for a second on the bright barrel of a pistol. 

He replaced it carefully. 

Clambering over the wall, he passed behind a dark farmhouse, and noticed wires running to the barn. He reasoned that where there was electricity there was probably a telephone also; at least, it was worth trying. 

But McArthur didn't need a telephone yet; he walked on cautiously through another field, until he arrived beside the shack where the truck was standing. 

This, the bootlegger's northern refuge, seemed deserted. With great care McArthur advanced toward the vehicle, and found it empty. What of the other building? That, too, had appeared dark from the cab, but the inventor wasn't certain.

He slipped noiselessly into the woods, to avoid the dim light cast from the lamp above the tablet, and groped his way forward. To his surprise, he emerged upon a railroad track, which swung in a long curve toward Salisburyport. For an instant he was puzzled. 

It couldn't be the main line north- No; it was the 

Tri-State division, direct to New York, winding across the countryside. Walking quietly on toward the curve of the tracks, McArthur found himself directly in the rear of the southern house. He paused in the woods beside the curve and studied the place. 

It was composed of two sections: one with a shingled roof, and a smaller wing extending toward the north.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

The only door visible was located at the end of the wing, with several steps ascending. In the main part, facing the railroad, was an uncurtained window, which was dark. Another window in the wing had shutters nailed tight with boards.

The hockey player had taken out his flash light, contemplating an investigation of the nearer room through the glass, when his attention was caught by a flickering radiance from the highway. A car was approaching from the south at great speed. As it drew near, it slowed with a shriek of brakes, pulling to a stop beside the driveway which led to the house. 

McArthur felt a

The machine was a large sedan. conviction that it was the one he had passed at Little

Two men alighted from the front seat and ran straight toward him, up the drive to the door in the wing of the shack. One of the pair knocked quietly. 

The inventor was distinctly surprised as a bolt shot back.

For a moment there was a murmur of voices. Both of the newcomers presently entered, and the door was bolted again, but no light appeared in the larger room where the window faced the tracks. McArthur put away his flash light and waited.

In less than five minutes the pair came out, and a third man accompanied them to the door. The glowing end of his cigar was visible to the hockey player. There were a few more words on the doorstep; then the two men from the sedan hurried back to the road. The other stood on the threshold watching them, his cigar still burning.

THE BOOTLEGGER'S SHACKS 211 

The churn of the starter came sharply. The big car pulled into the drive, preparatory to turning around. 

All at once the headlights, which had been dimmed while the machine was parked, flashed bright, and their rays illumined the side of the old house, penetrating clear to the railroad tracks. McArthur caught his breath and crouched low among the trees. 

Had the man in the doorway observed him?

Apparently he had not, for after a moment he went inside and the bolt was slammed, while the sedan gathered speed and disappeared in the direction of Salisburyport. The inventor blinked in satisfaction as he guessed the men's reason for returning. His strategy with the Gray Ghost had been successful. 

He waited, hoping to see a light through the window, but still none was visible. After a short time he was again surprised by the clatter of the bolt.

A man of medium stature came out, wearing a cap and a short overcoat. McArthur could not discern whether he was the one who had been standing in the door. As the other was descending the steps he stopped and called back in a thick, foreign voice: "Fasten de door, Birdie-when I come I'll knock six times." 

Birdie! The inventor's gaze sharpened.

He saw the man walk away from the house in the direction of the shack across the State line, but lost sight of him in the dark after he had gone a short distance. His heart was pounding wildly. This was his big chance, his one chance-if the other didn't return too soon! He listened hopefully but vainly for the sound of the motor truck starting. Two minutes

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

dragged past-three-four. Then McArthur rose with decision and stepped silently across the clearing to the door. He crept up the broken steps. 

His right hand was clenched in his pocket. With his left he tapped six times.

The response was immediate. In a thicket fringing the tracks a gun barked savagely, then again, and again, and bullets splintered the woodwork by McArthur's head. He flung himself to shelter behind the steps. 

Here he was safe for the moment, unless his assailant should alter his position, or unless the bootlegger inside the house- He checked his thoughts, blinking. "McArthur, you colossal dumb-bell!" he muttered. 

His chance? It was Birdie Zevitas who had played the game. Zevitas had caught sight of him, of course, by that sudden, strong glare from the headlights-he had invited him to "knock six times"—this was Birdie Zevitas who had shot at him from the thicket! He would scarcely have fired at the door had there been any one in the house to answer the knocking. 

It was evident that Zevitas wasn't hampered by the gangsters' necessity of making it appear to be an accident! 

The inventor glanced around warily. He couldn't crouch here by the steps indefinitely. He might sprint across the clearing, aided by the darkness- But in his heart McArthur knew that the bootlegger was waiting for this; and above everything else he loved the unexpected.

Reaching up, he felt for the knob, found the door unbolted as he anticipated-and with a quick effort, lunged across the threshold.

CHAPTER XXXII THE DEATH PERIOD 

SPRINGING erect, the inventor closed the door and quietly pushed the bolt.

He drew a breath. His plan to elude Zevitas was to leave the house by the other door-if there was another. Turning in the dark to investigate, he found his passage blocked by walls on every side. 

A rubber coat and an oilskin hung from hooks on the left; and opposite stood an old-fashioned refrigerator, long in disuse if he could judge by the layer of dust on the top. Straight in front were more hooks. 

But presently McArthur found an inner door. The place was a vestibule, and this was why he had seen no light upon the arrival and departure of the two gangsters.

He stumbled into another pitch-black space; and here, hazarding his flash light to gain time, he saw something which caused him to stop with a low whistle. There was liquor in the shack to-night—not much, true, only three cases, which could be carried away at a minute's notice-but enough to satisfy the law. And there was more than a liquor charge to halt the bootlegger's activities now-an assault with intent to kill. 

The presence of any quantity at all was a revelation to McArthur. Evidently Zevitas had felt perfectly secure because the liquor couldn't be seized legally un- 213

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

less it was first observed on the premises and a warrant obtained. The inventor hesitated. Did Zevitas now realize that he had forfeited this immunity by his attack from the thicket? If so, what action would he take?

The light had shown McArthur other details-heavy curtains, tacked to the boarded windows. It was plain that the bootlegger didn't wish any light to show from this part of the house, where undoubtedly, he often worked far into the morning hours. A few feet ahead, McArthur had glimpsed another door, and he guessed that it led to the larger room which he had seen from the railroad. He moved on, anxious to learn if there was another means of exit from the shack-or of entrance for Birdie Zevitas! Entering the farther room, he soon found his question answered. There was a door leading out, facing south, but it was nailed and reënforced by heavy planks. 

Trapped, the hockey player turned and listened. He had left the way open clear to the vestibule, and he felt sure that he would hear Zevitas the instant that he touched the bolted door. But was the latter aware yet that he had entered the house? Did he fancy that he was still cowering behind the steps?

Perhaps the next attack would come from a window. 

There were four in the large room, all without curtains, giving a view on every side except the north, where the wing extended. McArthur moved toward the blind wall. Surely, inside in the darkness looking out, he would be at an advantage over his adversary.

As he tiptoed across the floor, his head came in contact with some hanging object, and there was a

THE DEATH PERIOD 

215 Startled, flash of bright illumination in the room. the inventor drew back; and an instant later the same object, swinging from the impact, struck his head again. 

There was another quick, strong flash of light.

In a moment he understood what had happened. An electric drop-lamp was suspended on a cord, and the bulb or the wire was defective. When struck or shaken, the light burned momentarily. Avoiding it carefully this time, McArthur crossed and took a position at the wall, not far from the door to the wing. There was no doubt now that Zevitas knew he was inside-not after those two bright flashes through the windows. 

The hockey player smiled and shook his head. "He's certainly played rings around you to-night, McArthur. He's driven you into overtime-and if you don't get him first, he'll get you."

The thought involved another consideration. Had the bootlegger made arrangements already for assistance from others? He had permitted the gangsters in the sedan to go away; he hadn't informed them of his discovery. But at that instant he couldn't very well have told them, without betraying his knowledge and spoiling his chance to set a trap. Had he since conveyed word to other friends?

No; because there wasn't any telephone in the shack. 

The rays of McArthur's light in the smaller room, the two quick flashes from the electric fixture here, had answered that. This uncurtained room was completely bare of furnishings, with a few odds and ends piled in the corners. It was evident that Zevitas used the house only as a hiding place for liquor, not as his headquarters.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

There remained the possibility that the other had gone for aid since he, McArthur, had entered the shack. 

But this seemed very unlikely, after the attack from the thicket. The inventor knew that he was now a source of great peril to the bootlegger. His testimony, if given, might convict the latter of attempted murder; and Zevitas, of course, knew it equally well. 

McArthur was beginning to realize that he had encountered a far more dangerous foe than any of Dr. 

Marsh's hired crew-a confirmed criminal, shrewd, cunning, and merciless. It wasn't an inviting assignment, after all, to oppose this man on his home grounds. 

With every sense alert, the inventor stood rigid, his back to the wall. He watched the hazy oblong of each window.

All at once his body stiffened and his hand went instinctively to the pocket of his coat. He would have sworn that he had heard a faint sound from the opposite corner, inside the room, as of some one moving stealthily. But in a second he had calmed his nerves.

It was impossible. He had assured himself that he was alone in the shack. Had any one else been there, the two flashes of light would have revealed him. Besides, the bootlegger wouldn't have sent bullets into the door if there had been an accomplice inside. 

After a few moments, however, McArthur felt again that odd sensation of another presence-a sound, so soft that he could scarcely be sure he had heard itthis time from a slightly different direction. The full significance gripped him as he listened. There was some one in the room. Birdie Zevitas was in the room.

217 Stella's words, forgotten, came back to him: there was a tunnel between the two houses. 

With the realization, McArthur began edging his arm upward, carefully, silently, until his fingers reached his pocket and closed on the cold metal of his pistol. 

It was the first shot to win now-that much was certain. Zevitas had learned of his whereabouts by the flashing droplight; he was exploring the room at present, searching for him, moving probably on hands and knees to avoid having his own shadow outlined against the windows. Thankful that he himself had chosen the darkest possible background, the inventor sank to a crouching position. His left knee, injured in hockey, gave a slight snap as he did so, and for a second he held his breath, waiting to learn if the sound had betrayed him. But there was no move by his antagonist.

and risk involved in coming back by the underground passage?

How long would it take them to realize that they had been outwitted? McArthur believed that he was safe from these men for thirty minutes. And then suddenly he cut short his reckoning-this game wasn't going to last thirty minutes, not by any means-it would be over in ten, or in twelve or fifteen at the most. 

He had forgotten the railroad track, close by the rear of the house. McArthur hadn't looked at his watch since before twelve-thirty, but he had learned to estimate time with great precision. In ten minutes now, very little more, the Owl's Wing Express would come roaring up the track toward Salisburyport, bound for New York. On the curve, its giant headlight would shine in through the window at the back, flooding the room with radiance. And-McArthur saw it all too plainly as the train would swing the long bend, it would light his end of the room first!

Zevitas, the killer, crouching somewhere at the opposite end, probably foresaw this as clearly as he. It was a time for desperate tactics. Groping behind him, McArthur found a number of small, dusty blocks of wood on the floor. They felt like the sawed ends of lumber. He picked up one softly and tossed it diagonally across the room, toward what he believed to be a neutral corner.

219 silence followed. Birdie Zevitas wasn't to be drawn by such a ruse.

powerful drivers and the pounding of trucks grew distinct; and, as the Owl's Wing Express thundered closer, a monster gleaming eye appeared, swinging the curve.

He saw at once that she must be silenced at any cost.

CHAPTER XXXIV HIGH INSURANCE RATES VINCENT PROUL KEENAN really was not eager to take Dr. Marsh's case. He had long since made his reputation as the greatest criminal lawyer in the State, and from this he derived ample satisfaction. In recent years, however, he had begun to find himself the possessor of another kind of fame-that of the biggest gang lawyer, defender of the most vicious lawbreakers of all types.

There had also been a suggestion that his methods of winning cases were sometimes open to questionalthough it should be added, in justice to Keenan, that no proof of this had ever been forthcoming. The famous attorney had passed fifty; there were edges of gray in his hair, wrinkles appearing in his hawklike face, although his blue eyes were still sharp and piercing; he had become a wealthy man; and, secretly, had begun to grow weary of a fame which associated him always with the lowest dregs of society. 

For this reason-although he was still technically the gang lawyer-he looked with disfavor upon Dr. 

Marsh's proposition, realizing that it could only bring an added portion of unsavory publicity. To avoid accepting the case, he informed the physician that he would expect a retaining fee of thirty thousand dollars. 228

HIGH INSURANCE RATES 

229 Early the next morning the money was laid on his desk. "So," he bemoaned later, addressing his assistant, "what was I to do?"

His first real conference with Dr. Marsh was held on the afternoon of that day. When the other was adınitted to the opposite end of his spacious office and the heavy door latched, Keenan rose belligerently. If you think you can make me a party to any more of your dirty frame-ups-" "Now, wait a minute, Keenan-" pleaded Dr. Marsh.

His tone was weak. He seemed a broken and tottering figure, twenty years older than he had appeared upon his last visit to the attorney. The ruddy tan of his features, which in the past had preserved an appearance almost of youth, had disappeared overnight; his face was a poisonous vision, flabby as the rest of his body, and dull white like paste. "Keenan, old man, my back's to the wall. With you to help me, I can pull out of it-I can put it across-" "Put it across, can you?" thundered the other. "You've put about enough across with me to help you! Why, you miserable worm! You slimy reptile! 

Sit down and have a drink!"

CHAPTER XXXV DR. MARSH SCORES 

WEARING one of his most fashionable suits and a new topcoat-for the morning was mild-Kendall McArthur ascended the wide steps of the central courthouse on Friday and went inside, keeping watch for Nick Lontos. In the corridor on the first floor, near the lower criminal courts, groups of loungers muttered as he passed, a gambler made a sneering remark, a policeman scowled darkly. Upstairs were other knots of men; a pair of lawyers grinned and winked at friends near by. It wasn't only in hockey that Dizzy McArthur was jeered and "ridden" by the crowd. 

Not all of the idlers were at court from mere curiosity. The inventor knew that many were there with a definite purpose. It was known that Lontos had a case to be heard that day; and denizens of gangland, lawbreakers of almost every description, had gathered to observe the identity of his agents.

Arriving at the alcove outside the equity sessions, McArthur found his friend standing by one of the settees for witnesses. By his side sat a serious-faced youth with slightly stooped shoulders, whose name was Bishop, and a girl who had delicate, rather pale complexion and timid blue eyes. Across the alcove slouched

CHAPTER XXXVI THE MISSING WITNESS 

It was seldom indeed that Dizzy McArthur felt apprehension; he didn't quite understand fear; but when this faltering message came over the wire, a cold hand seemed to lay its fingers for an instant upon his heart, causing it to jump and stumble. There were few men in the State who knew Dr. Marsh's vast system more thoroughly than the inventor-few who were better acquainted with the various illicit ramifications of that system, with other promoters of lawlessness who were allied with the physician. In McArthur's mind, the news from Lontos was suggestive of the most terrible possibilities.

He kept all this out of his voice, however, as he replied quietly: "I'll be right over, Nick."

Running out of the office, McArthur sprang to the driver's seat of the hired sedan without a word of explanation to either of the other men. While the clerk stared, he plunged his foot upon the starter, coaxed the motor to a roar, sent a harsh blast of the horn, and released the clutch. CHAPTER XXXVII WHERE IS THE RED INN? 

"WE must drop the case," declared Nick Lontos, with decision. 

He made this announcement after a half hour of moody contemplation, and reiterated it as he rose for his coat and hat. His face was firm-set, bitter with anxiety. "There is no alternative; it might cost Helen her life to go on. But damn that blackguard! Damn him! 

Some day, I promise you, Mac, I will settle with him for this!" 

McArthur did not argue with him that evening. He knew Lontos, and he realized that such procedure would serve only to fasten him to his purpose immovably.

In the morning there might be a chance-just a chance —that the lawyer, with his brain cleared by a night's rest, could be persuaded to view the problem in a different light. 

For his own part, McArthur didn't for an instant underestimate the peril to Helen Bruce. He had recognized the true gravity of the matter from the first, even more keenly than his friend. But in McArthur's mind there was another issue, equally vital, which he balanced against the girl's fate. This, also, was concerned with a woman's welfare-not a young woman, it was trueone with traces of gray in her hair, and with her beauty 248

WHERE IS THE RED INN? 

249 marred by lines of suffering and by sad, haunted eyes which told of fear. Mrs. Marsh was in danger as great as Helen's-a woman legally enslaved to a fiend. 

Of course Nick Lontos couldn't be expected to regard

Mrs. Marsh's case and Helen Bruce's with equal concern, but the hockey player honestly felt that there was more at stake in the former than in the latter. If the bill to free Mrs. Marsh should be withdrawn, the prosecution of the dope king must be abandoned also-and instead of Helen Bruce there soon might be a score of others, cases unknown to the public, victims of the systemized "mob" of the underworld-like one whom McArthur had known, and whom he had called the little girl of the night.

Then, too, in the inventor's mind was the knowledge that the withdrawal of the bill in equity would be an admission of surrender. The same craving for combat which had led McArthur to join the poorest team in the hockey league rather than one of the other clubs that had sought his services, the same spirit which had caused him to ally himself with Lontos, was now crying out against accepting even a temporary defeat. "Don't you see, Nick," he asked earnestly, at his friend's office, in the morning, "that if we give in to Marsh on this, he'll have a psychological edge on us in the future?"

The other shook his head. "I am not concerned with psychology at present, Mac. My first duty is to Miss 

Bruce and her family." "Well, then, taking it solely from that angle-what assurance have you that Miss Bruce will be set at liberty even after you've dropped the case?"

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"I admit that I have none," Lontos agreed, regarding him steadily. "But if I don't drop it-" "Suppose you do? Can you reopen it later?" "Surely. In due time I can maintain another bill alleging the same conspiracy-" "Well, doesn't Dr. Marsh realize that? Doesn't he know you can start again when Miss Bruce is safe? 

When you pin your faith on his agent's promise, Nick, you are taking a grave risk." "Yes; I know." The other drew a long breath. "But what can I do?" He sat gazing through the window at the cold, drizzling rain. "There is wisdom in what you say, Mac-in the determined course you advise. If I had anything whatever to help me in my search for Helen-even the slightest clew-but in the dark as I am―" "You haven't forgotten what Mrs. Marsh said about the Red Inn?" "No; but where is the place? See here!"—he caught up a slip of paper. "I have made a most careful survey. There is no licensed establishment in the State known as the Red Inn. There are two Red Dragons, one Red Lantern, and one Red Lion; but none of these is of questionable character." "The name," his friend offered, "may refer to the color of the building. I'll make a bargain with you, Nick!" he added, rising. "Give me forty-eight hours before withdrawing your bill in equity, and I'll find the 

Red Inn."

The lawyer-investigator crossed the rug again, then turned, and there was a new light in his eyes. He hesitated.

The Pittsburgh Panthers were still in town, to wind up their regular season in a contest with the Independents. The visitors were fighting desperately for third place in the standing, as this meant participating in the big series known as the finals or the play-offs.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

In this series the three leading teams in the league would compete, and each starting anew, regardless of previous standings. Thus the team finishing third at the end of the regular season would have a chance for the national title. 

The Panthers played furiously that night, but they were no match for Tom Black and his favorite referee, Hale. The latter was careful to keep their ranks depleted by penalties. It was a new experience for Mc- 

Arthur. He was familiar enough with Hale and his methods, but this was the first time that his team had benefited by his work. It 

The whole procedure disgusted the inventor. wasn't a very satisfying way to win a place in the finals. 

Of course he understood why Tom Black wanted the Independents to gain the coveted position-because then he would have both of the big semi-final games at his own Ice Palace. But McArthur didn't see that there was anything he could do about it, now that the game had started.

When the final bell rang, police escorted Hale from the building to protect him from the aroused Pittsburgh players. McArthur made it a point to visit the

CHAPTER XXXVIII THE ROAD IN THE WOODS 

FOR the sixth time Nick Lontos clambered out of the hired coupe and directed his flash light upon a signboard. He muttered explosively as he returned. "Picnicking forbidden!" he scoffed in exasperation. "Is that the only notice they can post in the town of Wierham? Great Heavens-who would think of a picnic in such a God-forsaken hole? Picnicking forbidden! That, and South Rotting or some such place!"

The cold, wet, windy ride from the city had not improved Lontos's nerves, worn already to the breakingpoint. For the past two miles they had come through a black, winding road, thick with mud, without a dwelling or a building of any description, until a junction of five equally dark thoroughfares had balked them utterly. "I'm going to try branch number four," announced McArthur. "Oh, indeed! And which, pray, is branch number four ?" "Counting from the left. We may as well make a choice." "Do!" urged his companion, acidly. "And get us a little farther toward the end of the world! Are you out of your head to-night, Mac? Why attempt to follow directions from a half-wit?" 255

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"That chap didn't look so half-witted," replied the inventor, backing the car in the mud. "He advised us not to come here, didn't he? Yet he wouldn't explain why." "Oh, he was letting his imagination run wild. You'll find that the inn at the village, the Wierham Arms, is the one we should have investigated." "I tell you it isn't, Nick. There was a policeman standing there in the square-and Jimmie Brown was astonished when I told him a policeman happened along." "But how do you know a policeman did happen along? You're not logical." "All right; I'm not," agreed McArthur, patiently.

The road upon which they had turned led down a steep curve for a few hundred yards, then abruptly crossed a rickety wooden bridge that shook and rumbled. They saw the glint of the headlights on a muddy streamlet; and straight ahead the beams picked out a lonely, dull white house, at first glance dark and deserted, but with occasional sparks rising from one of its great chimneys. The hockey player drove on, around another curve bordered by stunted trees, until they found themselves approaching a swamp which had inundated the ruts made by tires. "There I told you so!" reproached Nick Lontos, bitterly. "Well, perhaps you'll tell me the right road-how about it?"

THE ROAD IN THE WOODS 

257 bridge he stopped in curiosity. There was a light on the top floor now, groping through a dusty window. "Wait!" commanded his employer. "Thank the

As they ascended the winding hill, they were surprised to meet a machine with dim side lamps and a yellow light on the roof. "A taxi, wasn't it?" asked Lontos. "Yes. With two passengers." 

"I thought so! Where can they be going, on this back road?"

At the fork McArthur made another choice, but this road finally trailed off to a grassy drive, and he abandoned it while his companion fumed. Returning again to the five corners, the inventor was thoughtful. It had occurred to him in a vague way that there was something almost sinister in the hasty extinguishing of the light in the old house below. Without saying anything to Lontos, he took the fourth branch once more. 

But upon this occasion they found their progress blocked by a pair of automobile lamps so bright that the road was actually invisible. There was a halt, each driver waiting for the other. "Put off your lights-you're blinding him, Mac." "I'll dim when he does," was the stubborn response.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

and approached deliberately. The glare revealed the man as smooth-shaven and lantern-jawed, wearing a rubber coat and hat. He came to the window and surveyed McArthur with shrewd, gray-blue eyes. "Lost your way?" was his salutation.

McArthur ventured. "W-why, yes!" said the chief. "Come to think of it, it was dark red when it was built, and up until about two years ago."

McArthur did not answer. He was considering the

THE ROAD IN THE WOODS 259 

probable reaction of his newspaper acquaintance, Jimmie Brown, to the "tip" that he had given him. Brown would undoubtedly continue his policy of neutrality, but it was quite possible that he would modify it to the extent of warning certain of his friends that they shouldn't go to the Red Inn. Thus an inference might soon be drawn that the mysterious Red Inn was in immediate danger from Lontos and his agents-an inference which, although false, would be none the less embarrassing in its consequences!

The road terminated abruptly, and they emerged facing a large, rambling gray building with a long line of electric lamps. "It iss not such a goot efening, gentlemen? Yes?"

CHAPTER XL A RAID IS PLANNED CAPTAIN LAPLANTE of the State police was a bullet headed man of forty, with a countenance so tanned that it was almost black, and a crop of stubby, graystreaked hair. He had a widespread reputation for honesty and fearlessness-and also for his startling outbursts of temper, in which he mingled the best colloquial American with scalding elaborations in his native tongue. "Oh-by all the lovely saints and bald-pated demons!" he exploded, with numerous qualifications not included, "is that the place you mean?"

He had been comparing Nick Lontos's diagram with a folding map. "Why-sure! Upon my soul-it never crossed my mind the other day when you asked about the Red Inn at Wierham. You see, I never heard this joint called by any name except the old Stapleton place-the Stapleton House before they lost their ticket. So they've got a new racket-the scamps!"-and he added further emphasis. "You've had experience with the place, then?" asked McArthur. "Damned tooting I've had experience with it!" returned LaPlante, giving him a glance from his coalblack eyes. "I raided the joint twice several years ago, 269

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

but never got the birds I had warrants for. So Yelland sent you to the Lake Park Tavern instead, eh?" "We saw no evidence," Lontos hastened, "of anything suspicious there. In fact, there was only one other party, quite respectable young people-two engaged couples, I should say-” "Married couples, Nick." "I beg pardon, Mac?" "The ladies were together in the rumble seat. Only married couples ride that way."

His employer gave him a glance of annoyance and turned with dignity to LaPlante. "As I was about to say, sir, it is quite clear that if Miss Bruce and the other young lady are at Wierham, they are at the old white house, not at the tavern." "I agree with you; and you can bet your best starched collar I'll raid the joint, on my own knowledge of its previous character. There won't be any trouble in getting a search warrant." 

Lontos jumped up. "If we can stage it to-night, captain," he cried eagerly, "I'll be able to place several men at your service, ready to assist. But now!" He turned, dropping his voice a little. "What do you think of this suggestion? Mr. McArthur believes it would be an excellent plan to have one agent inside the house upon some pretext before raiding. In that way, we might guard against any last-minute attempt to injure the captives." 

La Plante inclined his bristling head. "Yes; that's a good idea, but how the big-horned devil are you going to get a man in there? If they're keeping these two girls there, they'll not let any strangers in."

271 "But what about a girl? I have a very capable one, an experienced operative for detective agencies, who is due to arrive at my office this forenoon.
