The greatest crowd ever known in semi-professional hockey besieged the Ice Palace that evening. At midafternoon a line had formed; and by five o'clock this had become a double line and had wound along South State Street and over the railroad bridge until it obstructed traffic on Wentworth Avenue. These were the standing-room customers; twelve thousand seats already had been sold at advanced prices, many issued to speculators on a fifty-fifty basis. Long after the limit set by the building commissioner had been passed, the human flood kept pouring in, pushing, jamming in, until at last the packed walls would hold no more, and those still outside were dispersed in much the same manner that a detachment of cavalry might have dispersed an unruly mob in Russia. "We're citizens, the same as those who have tickets!" 

McArthur heard one man shout-but the police took little heed of this.

Within, it was a scene of indescribable confusion. 

Patrons fought madly to reach their seats; the aisles were jammed tight; men were perched high on the big steel beams overhead and on the base of the bandstand; and many had broken the glass of windows to

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

obtain a foothold above the mass. To the inventor, the interior of the building seemed to be one great, oval, seething pot, alive with tension and hostility. 

In the Independents' little room below, he dressed with his customary care. He brushed his dull blue uniform, carefully parted his hair, and put on his old white baseball cap. Around him the other men were silent and stern-all except Donovan, the manager, who stood nervously chewing gum by the door. 

McArthur's heart was with these players. It didn't matter so much to him-although it hadn't been easy to be taunted and hooted all winter while others received the cheers. He had had his triumphs in the past, many of them. But there was little Dick Adams, whose wind was failing and who probably wasn't going to be signed for another season; Getchell, the goalie, fortytwo years old, who had never played on a winning team; and Bob Drew, a mere schoolboy.

They had all heard the comment-that the Independents were only in the big series by preference of the leading teams, who knew they could beat them at will. McArthur had helped to bring this little group up from the bottom, and his heart was with them now. "All right, gang-let's go!" said Dan Donovan gruffly.

With Getchell leading, they filed down the runway and skated out on the glistening surface. A sudden tumult rose, a scattering of applause mingled with catcalls and jeers. While the other players took practice shots at the cage, McArthur stood quietly apart, beside the wire screen at the end of the rink, watching his team-mates and listening to the music from the band.

A RAID IS PLANNED 

275 

A great, throbbing roar went up, and he observed the maroon-clad Athletic Club group emerging, skating out toward the opposite goal. He knew his opponents' strength. In Dalrymple they had a wonderful pokechecker; Schummell was the best goal-tend in the league; and there were Armstrong and Queen, a pair of thugs on skates.

In that whole vast assemblage every voice seemed raised in encouragement of the Athletic Club. As the inventor blinked up at the great bowl of faces, many noticed him and flung down a taunting chorus: "Boo!" "You'll get yours this time, Dizzy Mac!" "Better anchor that lid on-they'll scalp you to-night!" "Boo-oo!" "'S all over, Dizzy!"

Then all at once rose the strains of the national anthem; and in a second the wild throng hushed to silence, with row upon row of bare heads in a massive circle above the players.

The last notes died away; a mighty din shook the building anew; the referee-in-chief blew his whistle, dropped the puck, and the first semi-final game was on!

CHAPTER XLI TRIUMPH AND DISASTER A RATTLE of cheering broke forth as Bill Queen secured the puck after the face-off and charged down the ice. Swinging to the left of Dick Adams, he came on, his long, pointed face grimly set. McArthur reached out and poked the puck cleanly, then ducked his head as Queen retaliated with a swing of his stick. 

Picking up speed, the inventor skated back in a zigzag course until he in turn was checked by Dalrymple. 

The teams fought spiritedly for several minutes, each striving for the opening "jump," while the crowd above voiced clamorous approval. Twice Dalrymple stole the disk cleverly and flashed down the center of the rink with a wing beside him, while the tumult swelled to a roar, but Getchell, the bald goal-tend, coolly kicked the shots aside. 

Following one of these sallies, young Bob Drew of the Independents hooked the puck and broke away so fast that he skated clear to the maroon defense, where he let go a whistling drive which whanged against the back-boards. Then, almost exactly five minutes from the start, came the occurrence that stunned the immense throng and left them gasping.

Dick Adams was checked at mid-ice, and McArthur, who was following, scooped the loose puck and went flying down the right lane, dodging a vicious jab by 276

TRIUMPH AND DISASTER 

277 Queen. He lost control of the rubber pellet and sped behind the goal after it, pursued closely by his adversary. The disk lay near the end boards; and the inventor, whose winking eyes were upon it, caught a half glimpse of another maroon shape suddenly looming from the front. In a flash he sensed it—an old trick of Armstrong and Queen-they intended to make his body a bumper for a wicked collision involving also the boards and the rusty wire screen above them. 

He sprang aside, tumbling against the back of the goal-net.

There was a crash as the two men came together. 

Both sprawled on the ice. Ignoring them, McArthur darted forward, still blinking as his glance sought the small black object trickling toward the corner of the rink. A stride ahead of the nearer defense man, he reached it.

Out in front was Bob Drew, covered, and beyond was Adams. Swift and hard McArthur shot the puck; it cracked sharply on Adams' stick; Schummell of the 

Athletic Club hurled himself flat in the mouth of the cage-and behind him the goal umpire's white flag switched aloft.

In the building there was silence. The massed thousands above the rink sat bewildered, scarcely realizing that a score had been made.

The maroon-clad players, too, seemed dazed, and resumed play listlessly. A minute later McArthur carried again to the defense, where he sent a terrific shot which drummed against Schummell's pads. During the latter half of the period, he and his team-mates continued battering the Athletic Club's goal.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"Now play defense-play defense!" Dan Donovan exhorted in the dressing room. "That goal will look big if ya play defense!"

But out on the rink again, McArthur continued his thrilling dashes. He had tasted success, and he was eager for more. He believed that the best defense was a determined and unexpected attack; and with face set and eyes glittering he carried on. The throng rose in amazement as again and again he eluded his hard-checking opponents.

His breath had begun to come in gasps, for he had set a wicked pace-much too fast for any of his mates except young Drew-but the flash of his shining skates kept swift and firm. Teaming with Drew, he spread the maroon points and reeled through, straight up to the front of the goal, but the young player missed his pass before an open net. A few minutes later the same pair made another charge, splitting the defense wide. 

Bob Drew skated in upon the goal-tend and drove for the corner of the cage with all his strength. By a wild effort Schummell threw his body outward and downward, face foremost. The puck thumped his padded chest and rebounded, spinning in front of himand this time it was McArthur, the showman, butt of the hoots and jeers throughout the season, who rapped it into the net. 

In desperation the Athletic Club men began tripping 

McArthur, cross-checking him without penalty, jabbing at him with their sticks. Repeatedly he was sent headlong, twice he was crashed against the boards, and once he turned a half somersault with such force

279 that his white cap was knocked from his head and his stick was smashed.

Didn't you hear what I said, Mac?" "We're playing them off their feet," young Drew protested. "Ye're playin' them clean offa the ice altogether!" admitted the manager. "But what of it? I told ya to make them carry the game!"

The inventor sat panting in a corner, scarcely listening. His thoughts were at Wierham just then. "I wonder how Nick's score stands," he said to himself.

In the dressing room Donovan swayed his players with his logic, but out on the ice it was Dizzy McArthur who led them, and the third period was scarcely under way before they were startling the crowd with a fresh offensive. The inventor knew only one strategy, one creed, and that was to attack. Again and again he circled behind his own cage, blinking, noting the positions of the men up the ice as a chess player studies his board, then launching forth in a weaving, spinning advance. The maroon stars swarmed madly around him, bumped him, slashed him, but always a path opened and he reeled on.

Starting in back of his net, he carried the puck three times past the entire Athletic Club team, right up to the mouth of the goal, but on each occasion by some miracle Schummell saved. Above them the packed house rocked and roared; the sound grew to deafening

volume. It had been a shout of wonder at first, of hostility to McArthur, but now when he swung from his end of the rink and plunged up the center of the surface, the great throng shrieked and screamed until the building trembled. "Go on, Mac!" "Go on, Dizzy boy!" "Go on! 

Go on!" they volleyed down through cracked throats -for all the world loves a battler. 

Straight up the ice he came once again, bluffed a pass, completely bewildered the defense pair, coasted in upon the goalie-and his shot sent the rubber disk fluttering against the strings like an imprisoned bird!

Then Eddie Armstrong brutally raised his bludgeon and brought it crashing down over McArthur's head; but the blow fell on the latter's own stick, shattering it he had seen it coming. Desperate, wild-eyed, the 

Athletic Club men fought in frenzy, using every trick they knew to keep the inventor from adding to the score. "Stop him!" "Spill him!" "Slam him!" implored their supporters.

At last the faint sound of the bell crept through the din. The crowd slowly dispersed, grappling with the stupendous fact that Donovan's club had practically clinched the right to battle for the national championship.

Nick Lontos had not returned when McArthur hurried to the office on Water Street; neither was he at his home up-town. But as the inventor arrived at his own apartment the telephone was ringing. "I'll answer it, Wing," he told the Chinese boy.

McArthur left his friend's home with a heavy heart.

It was up to him now, the blame for it all-not only because of Stella Towne's unsatisfactory performance, but because he had restrained Lontos from abandoning his purpose at the first. He had taken his stand because of his inevitable longing to be the aggressor, to attack. And now that he had gambled, had found the 

Red Inn but not Helen Bruce-would the course that he had chosen lead to dire results? 

Stella's failure, too, was a bitter disappointment.

In his heart he knew why he had urged his employer to give her a fresh chance-it was because of another girl, a little girl of the underworld who had never had a second chance until it was too late. It was as if 

Stella had traded upon his sympathy, aroused by his experience with the little girl of the night.

Nothing was accomplished in connection with the case during the following day. Another immense crowd watched the Independents play a dogged game of defense against the frantic efforts of the maroon-clad team. Forming a stanch pyramid at their end of the rink, poke-checking and body-checking indomitably, they held their opponents at bay. Once in the second period Dalrymple wormed his way through the mass and got off a quick back-hand shot which beat Getchell for the only score of the game, but a one-goal victory wasn't nearly enough to save the 

Athletic Club.

CHAPTER XLIV MC ARTHUR WORKS ALONE 

It was Friday; and the St. Croix team of New York, league-leaders throughout the regular season, had arrived for the first game of the finals; and Dizzy Mc- 

Arthur was carefully planning the details of a new offensive effort-but not in hockey.

He was content to let the evening's contest take care of itself; for from the first he had hesitated to accept Nick Lontos' opinion that the trail to the house at Wierham was entirely false, and the discovery and identification of Elinor Sheppard's body more than forty miles distant had merely confirmed his own belief. 

He didn't tell his employer what he suspected, for the latter's nerves weren't in condition to withstand further shocks. McArthur's theory was distinctly alarming-and in his mind it accounted satisfactorily for the sudden disappearance of Stella Towne, as well as for her peculiar conduct at the time of the raid. 

When he found it necessary to question his friend again, he did so cautiously. "In relating the details of searching the house, Nick, you spoke of two men who were with the Stapleton woman-do you remember?" "Yes. What about them ?” "I wish you'd describe them." "Why, one was a villainous-looking fellow of forty- 292

MCARTHUR WORKS ALONE 293 

five-Captain LaPlante said he was Maggie Stapleton's brother-and the other was a young giant, quite smartly dressed, with a florid face." "I suppose neither was one of the pair who stared at Miss Bruce that morning in the courthouse?" "Oh, no!" The other's tone was positive. "Well, then, please crack your brain carefully and tell me if you've ever encountered either of the Stapleton pair here in the underworld of the city.” "I'm quite certain that I haven't," replied Lontos.

It was plain that he wondered why McArthur stressed the point. "All right," said the inventor, very thoughtfully. "Now, just how long did your party spend searching the premises?" "Oh, we did the work most thoroughly and painstakingly. We spent nearly an hour in the cellar alone." "Good-God!" his friend protested softly. But he didn't let Nick Lontos hear this. 

That night, with the Ice Palace once again packed to capacity, the Independents met the St. Croix team, and found to their astonishment that they had suddenly made more frenzied supporters than the Athletic

Club had ever possessed. From start to finish the building was in one vast, thundering roar which seemed to pick up the local players as if upon the crest of a mighty wave, sweeping them on.

McArthur didn't figure in the scoring, for it wasn't his night, and he was too closely covered by the visitors' talented Canadian forwards; but twice young

Bob Drew received the puck at the end of brilliant combination rushes and sent it whizzing into the cage.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

Toward the end, the St. Croix team's superior manpower began to tell; and Bert Kimball, their famous defense player, crashed through to jam a shot past

Getchell. But the last bell left the Independents with a one-goal lead to carry with them to New York.

Saturday passed; and McArthur was still busy planning his supreme offensive, his final attack. He was fully aware that his contemplated move, when made, would be decisive. If successful, it might attain an even greater goal than Nick Lontos had dreamed of. 

If unsuccessful-well, McArthur didn't care to think of that. "So LaPlante intends to go back and search the cellar by daylight, does he, McArthur?" the hockey player said to himself. "Well, not if you can prevent it!" 

And he went forthwith to the captain's office.

The latter received him courteously and apparently was glad that he had called, but expressed in no uncertain terms his disgust with the events of the evening at Wierham. "Whatever gave you the idea that the missing girls might be in that house, anyway, Mr. McArthur?" "Oh, that was merely a guess. We were eager for any possible clew. But, you know, there's quite definite information that the place is a resort for users of narcotic drugs. Do I understand that you're willing to make a second search of the premises?" "I'm going to, in the daytime!" was the positive response, reënforced by a French oath. "There's a hiding-place for dope there, or I miss my guess.' 99 "Well, captain, I'll make a bargain with you," McArthur offered, blinking at him in a speculative manner.

MCARTHUR WORKS ALONE 

295 "If you'll take me along next time and go at night, I'll guarantee to find the hide-away." 

The other's glance was curious for a second; then he dealt his knee a resounding slap. "Well, by all the great horned toads and little bullfrogs-I don't know what's in your head, Mr. Mac, but I'll take you up on that proposition! I warn you, however, that you'll not succeed in getting any one inside before we raid this time."

McArthur made a pretense of explaining, but gave him only a vague idea of what he was contemplating. 

He had said nothing at all to Nick Lontos about it, because he knew that his employer would veto such a method. The inventor felt that this was one case where 

Lontos should be given no opportunity to stand upon scruples, a case where success or failure might mean life or death, and where their opponents might be met at their own style of play!

It was tentatively agreed with the captain that the effort would be made on Monday evening. McArthur emphatically advised that the police bring sledge-hammers, crowbars, axes, and saws, and suggested that the lawbreakers probably would post watchers on the roads to guard against a second surprise. LaPlante promised that his party would descend upon the town without warning and would strike immediately.

McArthur took the midnight train to New York, where the deciding hockey game was to be played on

Sunday. He was torn between two obligations: to help Lontos unravel his tangle without further misfortune, and his duty to his stout-hearted little band of players who had fought their way from the cellar to

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

the finals. At sight of the sporting page in an evening paper, he shook his head. "So-it's 'the great Dizzy McArthur' now, is it?" he chuckled, blinking. "What a difference it makes when you win!"

CHAPTER XLV THE SUPREME GAMBLE 

HAD McArthur been at the Canton Cabaret in the South End at the hour when the train left, he might have observed two familiar figures ascending together to a private dining room on the second floor. One, a man of fifty who had a pallid, worn face and flabby hands, was well known at the resort; while the other, a stranger there, was somewhat younger, carelessly attired, with a lean, angular countenance and dark spectacles.

Evidently the pair had serious matters to discuss, for the elder man ordered the Chinese waiter not to intrude. "So you've decided at last that you can't work for pin money all your life?" he ventured, giving his companion a penetrating scrutiny from his dull, watery brown eyes. 

The other grinned. "I might have been willing to talk with you before," he admitted, "only those guys of yours were so crude about it. They were so damn crude I thought they might be working for Lontos, to test me! But"-he hesitated an instant-"I've got the straight info now, and I'm willing to listen to anything reasonable." "Do you mean," asked his host, softly, "that you are in a position to give me information pertaining to the pending case?" 297

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"I dare say I could give you plenty," returned the man with the dark glasses, unbuttoning his flaring green overcoat and lighting a cheap cigar. "Here-have a real smoke, while you're about it. 

And tell me what you know about the present plans." "Oh, I'm not too keen about telling it-" The man coolly accepted the proffered cigar and noted the donor's eagerness. "What I said was, I dare say I could give you plenty. For instance, I know just what night him and me are going together to do some scouting at Wierham. But maybe Wierham don't interest you no more." "How do you know this?" "He told me himself. And I guess I'm about the only one that does know it. He ain't told Lontos nothing about it." "No? I wonder why not?" "I dunno that"-with a shrug. "I suppose he wants the credit himself. But-" Again he hesitated oddly. "If I wanted to, I could fix it so I'd be the one to do the driving when we go out there. I can fix it. If you're still as keen as them guys said you was a month ago- "Oh. So you'll be doing the driving?" mused the older man, in a very quiet, calculating tone. 

When they parted that night the man with the green overcoat had more money in his pocket than he had possessed for many a day. But before morning he had lost nearly all of it—at poker. 

The big hockey game in New York almost resembled a football contest. Again and again Bert Kimball, like

THE SUPREME GAMBLE 

299 a huge striped cat in his gold-barred uniform, hurled his one hundred and ninety-eight pounds of brawn at the Independents' stubborn defense. Repeatedly he smashed his way through, spilling his adversaries right and left, but upon every occasion he lost the puck in the mêlée. The bald goalie, Getchell, faced at last by his chance to play on a championship aggregation, offered the greatest effort of his career.

The game was scoreless; and when, finally, the clanging gong brought its note of doom to the New Yorkers' hopes, Kimball broke his stick in disgust and shook hands with his friend McArthur. "I'm glad you won it, if we couldn't," was his comment. 

The morning newspapers, narrating the capture of the national title by Donovan's amazing club, stated that Dizzy McArthur had received a severe gash from a St. Croix stick in the third period. The inventor smiled when he read this, for it was greatly exaggerated. 

At luncheon on Monday, McArthur took Nick Lontos partly into his confidence, telling him of Captain 

LaPlante's decision to make a second raid upon the house at Wierham, and of his own agreement to cooperate. He found his employer quite willing to take part in the affair, for it was believed that there were narcotics to be seized, and since Helen Bruce's disappearance this work had been neglected. 

Plans were made hastily. Bishop and Phillips were out of the city, leaving only three men to assist the 

State troopers. Two automobiles were made ready-

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

McArthur's big gray touring car and a hired roadster driven by Bowen.

Punctually at eight o'clock Lontos climbed into the Gray Ghost with McArthur, and they sped out over the smooth Pine Tree Turnpike at a pace so fast that Bowen had difficulty in following. At South Hocking they swung to the left, then along the straight stretch to Wierham—a run of thirty-two miles in fifty minutes. 

But as they were descending a grade almost within sight of the village, there was a sudden rasping sound from the gears, and the machine coasted to a standstill. "What's the trouble?" asked Lontos in alarm.

His friend shook his head dubiously. "It sounded like the rear axle, Nick."

He let in the clutch several times without result, then leaped out and signaled to Bowen, who applied his brakes. "A pretty mess this makes!" fumed their employer, snatching out his watch. "Can we get it repaired at the village?" "My dear fellow, a broken rear axle on an old boat like this means three days' work." "What? Well, why didn't you see to it before we started ?"

A stranger of nondescript appearance, who evidently had been seated on a stone wall near by, directed them to Temple's Garage at the town. The inventor locked his transmission, and, with Lontos, crowded into the roadster beside Bowen. At the service station McArthur arranged to have his car towed in from the highway. 

He paid in advance. "You might leave it outside

THE SUPREME GAMBLE 

301 here in the yard," he suggested, "where my own repair man can get it easily."

The three detectives then proceeded to a point on the road between Wierham and Putnam where they had agreed to meet the State police. "Twenty minutes past nine," observed Lontos, drawing a breath of relief. "The captain swore he'd have two carloads of troopers at this spot exactly on the stroke of ten."

Bowen parked the roadster on the dried grass at the side of the lonely road, extinguishing his lights, while McArthur seated himself on the running-board and lit a cigarette. He exhaled thoughtfully. 

The hockey player was counting the passing minutes.

He hadn't any thought of waiting for LaPlante's party; not if he could rely upon their arriving at the specified time. No!-he and Bowen were to make their attack first-apparently a reconnoitering expedition-but in reality a bold gamble that would settle the issue once and for all! 

As McArthur sat quietly smoking, his eyes were bright and cold as they had been more than a year earlier when he had first sworn to bring the dope king,

Dr. Marsh, to justice. Within thirty-five brief minutes now, the whole long battle might be ended, the outcome settled as if by the turn of a card. In the balance hung success, with all that it would mean to others or failure, final and absolute. 

And he would need luck-a full share of luck! Dizzy

McArthur didn't often want the road smoothed for him; he wasn't the sort who'd ask for favors; he'd rather line up with a weak team than with the league

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

leaders; yet in his heart was the hope that the Great 

Referee would give him an even break to-night. 

Finally he rose, tossed his cigarette on the macadamized surface, and carefully stamped out the glowing end -for there was a brisk breeze. He addressed Nick Lontos: "You'll have to meet the police cars here, of course, but Bowen and I are going ahead to do a little scouting." 

His employer caught his breath. "You certainly are not, Mac! Why, you'd give the alarm-they'd be prepared as they were before-” "Nick," pleaded the inventor, "I'll take the blame—” "No! You're not to go near the place until the troopers come!" "But I'll take the responsibility if anything goes wrong- 99 "No! I positively forbid it!" "Then listen, Nick!" returned McArthur, suddenly, fiercely, bending toward him in the dark. "If you want 

Helen Bruce to die as Miss Sheppard died, gasping out her life for want of air while we search blindly for her hiding-place-keep on forbidding it!" "Great-Heavens!" gasped Lontos, all at once weak and shaken. “Oh, my God- Go, Mac! I won't restrain you! Go!"

With Bowen driving, the roadster moved away.

CHAPTER XLVI THE SECRET OF THE RED INN 

THE quiet of the woods below the five corners was broken by the familiar rumble of the rickety bridge over the stream. And in an instant there followed more startling sounds-a heavy impact, the crackle of splintering timber, a single, sharp cry of alarm, and a soggy, slithering splash.

Then again there was silence, until a faint gurgle rose from the muddy water, a sputtered oath, and a wet form crawled over the bank of the streamlet and splashed up the front walk to the lonely white house near by. As the man approached, a window upstairs was thrown open, and the bony, repulsive face of a woman appeared in the dim light. "Hey! What's de matter-eh? Youse have a smash?" "Quick!" gasped the newcomer. "I went through the bridge and into the stream! Help me get him out of the car! Come down, quick!"

The woman did not comply with this request, but presently the door was unbarred and opened cautiously, and a massive, powerful man of twenty-five thrust out his head and shoulders. He was flashily dressed, and carried a lantern. "Quick!" urged the stranger. "He's in the drinkhelp me get him out!” 303

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"Dis ain't no life savin' station, bud," said the young giant.

But he checked himself as the other hurried closer. 

He raised his lantern. The newcomer was a smoothshaven man with dark glasses, wearing a fuzzy green overcoat that was dripping muddy water from the waist down. "Oh," he added then, in a thick, uncertain voice, "your moniker's Bowen-eh?" "You got me. 

But for Pete's sake, make it snappy -he ain't goin' to keep there all night! I tell you, I ran the can off the old bridge and tipped him down in the stream. Come quick!"

The big man in the doorway gave a low whistle and followed the other down the walk, across the road to the bridge. He whistled again as his lantern revealed one railing smashed away; then he swung the beams downward and caught sight of a roadster, lying partly on its side and stuck fast in the mud of the shallow streamlet. "Here-he's down here! Give me a lift with him-"

An oath of amazement escaped the ponderous youth as he went over the bank, and his light fell flickering on a stylish topcoat caked with slime, then a white, upturned face. A glimpse of a dark mustache and a strip of court-plaster on one cheek held him as if fascinated. "Boy!" he breathed. "Knocked him for a goaleh?” "Well, it oughter!" the driver exulted. "He's been under water for three minutes. For Pete's sake, give me a hand, will yer?"

THE SECRET OF THE RED INN 305 

Together they lifted the limp form. "Boy!-dis'll be worth a coupla grand apiece for us!" "Yeah? Well, when do I get mine?” "Don't worry, bud; you'll get yours. But, hell, we can't leave him lay out here! Some one might drive past-" The man with the dark glasses held back. "I ain't keen about goin' in there." "Aw, cut the clammy stuff! Come on wit' him!

Dis will be worth a coupla grand for both of us, I tell you! An' maybe the big brains won't make it look like an accident dis time!" he chuckled.

They paused, holding the dripping victim of the spill between them. "But-but listen, Jack. Is-is he in there?" "Naw-what d'you think-he ain't livin' here! I'll damn soon get him, though. What d'hell're you scared of-he'll pat you on de back for dis job!" "Yah-bring him in, damn him!" cackled the woman in the window. 

They stumbled over the threshold with their burden while she held open the door, then through a dim front hall to a shabby and musty parlor, where they laid the wet form upon a sofa. While an older man with a hard, inscrutable face entered the room and peered with satisfaction at the victim, the heavy youth strode to the hall and vigorously twirled the handle of a rural telephone. "Gimme seven ring twenty-one, make it snappy," he commanded. "Hello? Doc-that you? Well, say!-we got him here! Sure-"

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

The man in the green overcoat didn't hear the rest, for in the confusion he had slipped out of the parlor into a back portion of the hall and had found a carpeted stairway. With swift, lithe steps he darted to the top. In the darkness of an evil-smelling hall upstairs he risked his flash light, scanning the doors, searching for another flight leading to the attic. 

His dark glasses made vision difficult. He snatched them off: and now in the bright rays from his torch he blinked rapidly. 

The stairs to the third floor at last!-and below, the young man was still talking excitedly at the telephone. 

Taking the second flight three steps at a time, the detective paused and winked his light. Ahead were two straight-backed chairs and a blank wall covered with dust. At the left was a door, ajar. Turning his electric torch to the right, he discovered another door, closed and secured by a heavy bolt on the outside. On tiptoe he advanced and slid the bolt.

Instantly some peculiar sense told him that the room into which he peered was occupied. The bright circle fell on a window, with a succession of strong screens nailed inside, covering the glass. In one corner was a cot-a large bed in the center. 

As the light crept across the latter, a girl, fully dressed, suddenly sat upright with a gasp of fear. "Who who are you? What do you want?" "Be quiet, Miss Bruce!" requested McArthur. 

He stepped inside, leaving the door unlatched. "Are you alone here ?" "No-I'm here, too!" said another startled voice, and springs creaked from the cot in the corner.

THE SECRET OF THE RED INN 307 

"Miss Towne! Now, show me, quickly-where's the secret hide?" 

Helen Bruce had jumped to her feet, struggling for her self-composure. "Can-can we get out of the house, Mr. McArthur?" "I think we have a good chance. But in case anything should go wrong, show me the hide-away, quickly!" "Oh, yes!" The girl ran lightly to the door, followed by Stella Towne. "Is it outside of the room?" "Yes-in the hallway. Is it safe for us to go out there ?" "I don't think they'll hear us if we're careful. Their minds will be pretty well occupied for a few minutesunless they should start to look for Bowen." "Where's Bowen?" asked Stella. "Are the police here again?" "I'm Bowen, just at present; and the police are coming-I hope! Ha! I thought as much, Miss Bruce!"

The girl had hurried to one of the big chimneys which passed through the hallway, and was beckoning for his flash light. She began passing her hand rapidly over the bricks. "It's one of these about at the level of my head-" "Try this one," McArthur suggested, noticing finger marks.

All at once a whole section of the wide chimney swung outward on hinges with such weight that the young nurse was pushed violently aside, and the hockey player saw woodwork within.

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"H-mm! This isn't a chimney-it's an elevator shaft!"

Inside the brick inclosure, suspended by a heavy rope cable and pulley, was an oblong wooden receptacle not unlike the carriage of a dumb-waiter, except that this had a solid panel in front. "That's why the search party didn't find any hollow space. They'd expect a chimney to be hollow. And that's why you, Miss Towne, told the police to search upstairs, while the Stapleton people were alarmed only when the cellar was being searched. They let down this contrivance to the cellar-" "Oh, away below the cellar level, I think!" whispered 

Helen, shuddering. "We went creaking down and down and down! There must be a shaft, an extension of the chimney. "And see!" she added, as he flashed his torch again. "There are a few tiny breathing holes-I saw their lantern through them-but we were down there so long that all the carbon dioxide we exhaled accumulated in the bottom of the shaft-it's heavier than air, you know. And poor Elinor Sheppard, weakened by their drugs-" "Do you know why she was being kept here?" "Yes; she told me why. She came one night with some young people who attempted to drug her, and she had threatened exposure. Oh, it was a fearful experience, down in that stifling black shaft!"

McArthur nodded, listening carefully for sounds from below. "No wonder the Stapletons were uneasy about the cellar! They were afraid the police might open the

THE SECRET OF THE RED INN 309 

base of this chimney, or might possibly hear your cries." "It's been too dreadful-dreadful!" sobbed Helen, her nerves collapsing. "We hadn't anything-not even a chair left in our room, to break through the screens to the window-" "S-sh! Listen!" said McArthur. "Was that an automobile?"

He hastened softly through a room at the left to a dusty window that overlooked the front yard. “I hope it isn't the troopers— 99 "You hope it isn't?" gasped Stella, who was following. "Yes, yes-I don't want them to come too soon—”

He checked the words abruptly when he peered through the glass. The State police weren't going to arrive any too soon-perhaps not soon enough! Standing below, near the broken bridge, was a high-power, foreign sedan, and two men were running up the front walk. 

The inventor turned quietly. It was coming nowthe fight to the finish-and Heaven grant that the State troopers wouldn't be delayed! He blinked and led the two girls back to their room. "You'd better stay in here for the present," he suggested, "and I'll see if I can play goalie in the door." 

A bitter oath, a scream of fury, ascended from below-then a wild scuffle which ended as quickly as it began. McArthur shook his head. Bowen was a nervy fighter, but he wasn't any match for four heavy men.

The struggle was followed by another harsh scream in

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

the woman's voice and footsteps began pounding through the house.

They grew louder, first on the carpeted stairs, then in the hall just below. A swinging glow appeared as the heavy feet clumped on the second flight, and the hockey player craned his neck with a grim smile. There were three men ascending-the fourth evidently had stayed on Bowen's account- Good old Bowen!

The stumbling forms came nearer. One carried a lantern. "Stand back, please, gentlemen," requested McArthur, and he caught up one of the chairs in the hallway and flung it at the trio. 

The foremost man ducked his head, but the missile struck one of the others, and he flopped backward with a hoarse cry, tumbling to the bottom of the stairs. 

There was a crash of glass below. 

McArthur groped for the second chair. He couldn't find it quickly enough, and the remaining two men closed in, pinning him against the wall. It was a vicious battle in the dark. The two attackers aimed savage blows at the inventor, and several found their mark. Gradually he was being maneuvered out of his position at the door. One of his assailants landed a jolting body blow. McArthur's fist cracked sharply on the heavier man's jaw. The latter reeled aside, almost to the head of the stairs, but recovered and closed in with doubled fury, and the inventor was borne to his knees. 

The second attacker was kicking and jabbing from the side. A boot caught McArthur in the face, reopening the cut received in the hockey game, and

THE SECRET OF THE RED INN 311 

powerful fingers were reaching for his throat. He tore the hand away—

Then, all in a second, a hot, pungent breath fanned his face, and there were dancing lights in his eyes. A great fiery tongue licked the edge of the staircase-and into his mind leaped one thought-the broken lantern! 

The Red Inn was in flames.

CHAPTER XLVII  

THE two assailants discovered the fire at practically the same instant as McArthur. With cries of alarm, they turned and plunged down the stairway, which already was belching dense clouds of strangling smoke. 

The inventor lurched to his feet, choking and blinking-and a single glance down the stairs convinced him that the house was doomed. The blaze had started on the second floor, and the wind was driving it upward through the old dried timbers at tremendous speed. 

He swung around, calling sharply. 

Stella appeared in the door. flickering glare. "Mr. Mac! fainted!"

Quickly! Helen has

His heart sank as he glanced at the curtain of forked flame. Coughing, he ran to the window at the front of the house and smashed out the glass. The fire belched up from the floor below more fiercely than before.

The State troopers were outside now-swarming around the house, but they had no ladders-they were powerless to aid. A rope! McArthur thought of the cable of the secret elevator in the east chimney. But as he stumbled back, a fresh thought came to him.

Lifting Helen Bruce, he crowded her gently into the grim elevator inside the chimney. "You, too, Miss 312

Towne! We'll not leave you to suffocate this time. 

Let's make this contrivance save life for a change!" 

He banged the wooden panel of the compartment, then tugged at the heavy rope, lowering the device.

It seemed an endless time while the cable payed out. 

Certainly Helen had been correct-the bottom of the shaft was far below the level of the cellar- 

There at last! Pushing the chimney shut to keep out the flame and smoke, McArthur inverted Bowen's green overcoat, throwing the wet portion over his head. 

He drew a breath and staggered into the wall of fire, treading lightly on the tottering stairs.

He reached the next floor-the head of the carpeted flight-keenly conscious that he must not fail to escape in safety, or there would be no one to tell the rescuers where Helen and Stella were imprisoned. Descending farther, his foot struck a yielding shape, and he raised the wet coat from his head. One of his three assailants lay huddled on the steps, gasping. "This way out, sir-" said the hockey player. He gripped the man's coat collar and dragged him to the bottom. 

Outside at last, McArthur tumbled against Sergeant Cannon. "Hurry!" he panted. "Your sledge-hammers and crowbars! Smash open the base of the east chimney!" 

Hastily the troopers obeyed, demolishing the bricks, tearing them aside, while the fire roared and crackled above. An inner layer of wood was revealed. Captain 

LaPlante gave a cry of astonishment when he saw the gaping black hole and the cable extending beneath. 

Soon the opening was large enough to haul the ele-

vator upward. It was detached from the cable and carried outdoors, where the two prisoner-passengers were released. automobiles. He sagged heavily upon their arms; his gait was mechanical and listless.

Unexpectedly, as they approached the road, he whirled, taking the officers by surprise and eluding them. There was a bellow from LaPlante and sharp cries from the troopers as they flocked to search behind a pocket of pines, where the leaping flames threw the woods half in light, half in shadow. 

From across the bridge came the quiet churn of a starter. "The foreign sedan! Lively, men-after him!"

There was a roar of a motor, a pair of headlights leaped into life, and a tail lamp twinkled red as the Texas machine went snarling up the hill. The police sprang to their cars in pursuit. Nick Lontos, McArthur and Bowen crowded into a small coach with the captain. They ground uphill through the mud to the five corners, and there glimpsed a red speck on the narrow road to the village. Bouncing, lurching, swaying, they dashed on, but LaPlante shook his head as he bent above the wheel. "It'll be the very devil to catch that car!"

At the junction with the main highway Dr. Marsh again surprised them. He darted to the right, not to the left as they had expected. "He's going north to get out of the State!" surmised Lontos. "He is, eh?" the driver tossed over his shoulder. "Well, he'd better keep right on to Canada while he's about it! Look at him-look at him hammer it!" "Stop at the next garage!" McArthur exclaimed. "I've got a boat there that'll run him into the ground!"

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

"Here?" The captain jammed his brakes.

They tumbled out and crossed to the old gray touring car, piling in while McArthur raced his quiet engine. "But, Mac!-are you crazy? The rear axle is broken!" "Oh, so it is!" declared the hockey player, releasing the clutch so sharply that the machine took a wild leap forward with a grinding crunch of the tires. "Only it isn't." "Ho-ho!" said LaPlante in appreciation. "You figured they wouldn't be apt to tamper with your car if they thought it was broken down already-eh, Mr. Mac?" "But couldn't they have discovered the fraud?" asked Lontos. "Not while the transmission was locked."

Out on the straight, smooth road the gray car swung to the chase, the motor raising its voice to a gentle, insistent whine and the huge tires singing a mad song.

The speed mounted higher, still higher, until objects at the side of the road became indistinguishable and the wind whistled wildly-and then with a clattering crash McArthur threw the car into its fourth speed forward, its racing gear. "It's no use!" the captain barked, squinting at the tiny red tail light. "That foreign baby is good for ninety miles an hour." "Careful! Careful, Mac!"

But still the rush of wind came harder, wilder, shrieking past the dashboard in gusts that took the men's breath, until McArthur could feel its force grip-

ping and wrenching the car as he held the trembling wheel, and he wondered that it didn't crush the solid glass of the windshield and hurl the slivers in their faces. The road became a flying gray ribbon, straight at first, then curling and writhing as it reeled out of the night. "Mac! Mac! If we should skid now-" Nick

Lontos' voice was bitten off as McArthur laughed and pulled the hand throttle wide. 

Objects beside the highway jumped into the searching beams and flashed out again into the dark. From 

Bowen's throat burst a cheer-the red speck ahead was brighter, larger. "Ninety miles an hour, did you say, Captain La- 

Plante?" cried McArthur-and the wind cut the words out of his mouth. "Well, what do you think we're doing-hey, what?" 

The captain didn't try to answer. He was praying. "O-oooh-look at that! Oh, my God!" screamed 

Bowen, suddenly.

In place of the twinkling red pin-point, two bright balls spun crazily toward them for a half second, then completed a giddy circle—and swept around again, like a pair of flashing beacons. The lights disappeared completely, and an instant later the gusty breeze brought to their ears a terrific, rending, shattering crash, while the hills above the road seemed to catch up the fragments of sound and toss them back and forth between them. "I guess," said McArthur, slowing his big car gently -"I guess that probably lets us out of the case, doesn't it, Nick?"

THE SPORTSMAN-DETECTIVE 

But McArthur was wrong; for the framework of the foreign-made sedan was built of solid steel. Dr. 

Marsh spent weeks with his name on the danger list, but he lived to face a trial at which the great Keenan was unable to clear him.

Nick Lontos predicted the outcome later that night of the chase when they returned to the woods at Wierham to find two stark, blackened chimneys standing above a smoking ruin. "Whether the doctor lives or dies," he stated, "his organization is disrupted, for we'll have no difficulty now in showing that Mrs. Marsh has been the victim of conspiracy. They've spoiled their chance by molesting our witnesses, and she will be freed."

McArthur was learning from Stella Towne what had happened on the occasion of the first raid. "They must have seen me climb out the window, and feared that I'd been upstairs and seen the two girls after they were taken out of the elevator," she told him, "for, as I was walking up the road toward the police car, some one stepped out from the trees and threw a blanket over my head." "A peculiar break," was his comment. 

She looked at him quickly. "Is all of life just a game to you, Mr. Mac?" "Just a game," he admitted, blinking seriously. "A great game!-even better than hockey-but if only there were finals where we all could start over again!" 

For a moment his bright, dark eyes were deep in contemplation, wistful. 

And long after midnight, when the others had gone home, the trees and cliffs and giant bowlders bordering

the Pine Tree Turnpike were touched and tinged by dancing white beams; there came the beating, throbbing song of a motor, the prickle and whine of flying tires; and a pale-colored phantom cut through the darkness -Dizzy McArthur, once again speeding southward, alone in his big gray car.
